> **⚠ Generated by `generator/build-cross-reference.mjs` from the merged packages.**

**Task:** NEU-944 (SUB-11) · **Package version:** `1.0.0` · **Map version projected:** `0.1.0` · **Schema:** `1.0.0` · **Generated:** 2026-08-03

---

## What this view is

**The one hop.** For ANY technique in the map, this file resolves — in a single lookup, with no
further reads — its **node type**, **prerequisites** (intra-cluster, cross-cluster, and any
registered boundary-anchor terminal), **progression stage**, **difficulty**, **JavaScript
materiality**, **coverage verdict**, and **audit findings**, and marks **which of those are
binding and which are open**.

**This view is a PROJECTION and adjudicates nothing.** Every field is copied from the package
that owns it and names that owner. Status lives in exactly one place —
`../C005-dp-map-schema/adjudication/01_schema-decision-ledger.md`. This file never sets it.

## How to read a block

| Line | Meaning | Owner |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Type** | `node_kind` + `skill_type` (closed eight-value vocabulary) | NEU-933 `D-S1` |
| **Requires** | every prerequisite, by field. `intra` / `root` / `anchor` are DRAWN by the mapper; `cross` is REALIZED by NEU-939. An `anchor` line is a **SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, never a gap. | NEU-934–938 (drawn), NEU-939 (cross) |
| **Required by** | reverse edges — who cannot be taught before this | derived from the same edges |
| **Stage / difficulty** | NEU-940's applied values, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0` | NEU-940 |
| **JS** | materiality verdict, `rule_version 1.0.0` | NEU-941 |
| **Coverage** | the node's own `coverage` block | NEU-934–938; map-level verdict NEU-942 |
| **Findings** | every audit finding that lands on THIS node | NEU-943, NEU-939 |
| **Status** | the node's adjudicated status | the ledger, and only the ledger |

### The one thing you must read before you sequence anything

**`F-943-1` is CLOSED** (ledger `D-R4`). NEU-940's `progression_stage` and `prerequisite_depth`
had been computed against the **pre-NEU-939 graph** — before the cross-cluster edges existed —
leaving **26 of 179** depths wrong and **6** dependencies ordering backwards by stage. Those values
were **recomputed over the edge-complete graph** under ledger `D-R3` (NEU-954): **0 depth
mismatches, 0 stage inversions** remain, and `entry_gate` was re-derived on all 179.

**Consume accordingly (NEU-943 `05` §5, binding):**
**The stages and depths now agree with the graph, and either may be sequenced from.** The 25
cross-cluster edges are audited and correct, and no stage inverts across any of them. The graph's
topological order remains the strongest ordering — it exists, because the graph is acyclic — and
`prerequisite_depth` is now a **binding** value rather than an advisory one.

**The values remain `provisional` for a separate reason:** the creator progression-plausibility
review never ran (`D-P3`, map-wide). That is independent of `F-943-1` and is **not** closed by it.

See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` for the closure record and the remaining
open items.

### `cl-1.alignment-scoring-variants`

**Alignment scoring variants and what each does to the state** · aliases: *local alignment*, *Smith-Waterman shape*, *affine gap penalty*, *longest common substring*

> How the alignment shape deforms under different scoring rules, held as a compared set: arbitrary per-operation costs (weights change, state unchanged); local alignment, where a zero floor lets the alignment start anywhere and the answer becomes a max over all cells rather than a corner — the element-anchored pattern resurfacing in two dimensions; longest common SUBSTRING, where contiguity forces the same move; and affine gap costs, where a run of gaps costs less than its length and the state must therefore gain a small mode dimension recording which gap state the alignment is in. The last is the payoff: it is the state-machine pattern from Section 1 appearing inside an alignment, which is why the two sections are linked rather than parallel.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.edit-distance-dp` — Edit distance DP (Levenshtein)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.lcs-dp` — Longest common subsequence DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.state-machine-over-sequence` — Sequence DP with an auxiliary mode dimension (state-machine DP)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.best-ending-at-index-state-pattern` — Element-anchored state: "best ending exactly at i"
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-1.best-ending-at-index-state-pattern`

**Element-anchored state: "best ending exactly at i"** · aliases: *best ending here*, *endpoint-anchored state*, *suffix-anchored DP state*

> The state pattern that fixes the last element rather than ranging over a prefix — dp[i] means "the best structure that ENDS EXACTLY AT i", and the global answer is a max over all i rather than dp[n]. Why it is a distinct pattern and not a variant of the prefix-index state: anchoring the endpoint is what makes the transition LOCAL, because the anchored element is available to compare against candidate predecessors, which a prefix-optimum state has forgotten. This is the single insight shared by LIS and Kadane and the reason both are in this cluster; it is factored out as one node so the two techniques below share a prerequisite instead of each re-teaching it. The cost of the pattern, which is the thing learners miss: dp[n] is NOT the answer, and forgetting the final max is a distinct bug class.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-dp-pattern-catalogue` — The catalogue of linear/sequence DP state patterns
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-1.alignment-scoring-variants`, `cl-1.longest-increasing-subsequence-quadratic`, `cl-1.max-product-subarray-two-value-state`, `cl-1.maximum-subarray-kadane`

### `cl-1.build-prefix-aggregate`

**Build a prefix-aggregate array**

> Execute the fixed construction: size n+1 rather than n, seed the identity at index 0, sweep forward. The off-by-one discipline is the entire content — the 1-based-with-empty-prefix convention exists so that range queries need no special case at the left edge, and a learner who builds the n-sized version meets a boundary bug on every subsequent query rather than once.

**Type** — `skill` / `procedural`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.prefix-aggregate-recurrence` — The prefix-aggregate array as a DP recurrence
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.range-aggregate-by-difference`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E2`
- MATERIAL — the accumulator's exact range is three orders of magnitude smaller than the C++ default's, and this is the node that writes the accumulation. A C++ author reflexively declares the prefix array long long, exact to ~9.2e18, and stops thinking. JavaScript's Number is exact only to 2^53 ~ 9.0e15. A prefix sum over 1e6 elements of magnitude 1e9 lands at ~1e15 — INSIDE the bound, but within one order of magnitude of it, so the headroom a C++ author never has to reason about is nearly exhausted before the problem is even large. Past 2^53 the sum silently rounds rather than wrapping, so the array stays plausible while being wrong. Any prefix aggregate whose totals can exceed 2^53 requires BigInt. Recorded as correctness-risk, not blocking: the common case is correct, and a reachable one is not.

### `cl-1.circular-maximum-subarray`

**Circular maximum subarray: the complement reduction** · aliases: *circular maximum subarray*, *wrap-around Kadane*

> Maximum subarray on a circular array, solved not by a new recurrence but by a case split: the optimal subarray either does not wrap (plain Kadane) or does, in which case its COMPLEMENT is a non-wrapping subarray and is minimal, so the answer is total-minus-minimum-subarray. Mapped because the technique on display is REDUCTION rather than formulation — turning a new problem into two runs of a solved one — and because it carries a real degenerate trap: when every element is negative the wrap case's complement is the whole array, leaving an empty subarray, so that branch must be excluded rather than trusted. The same empty-solution defect as the Kadane boundary node, met in a place where it is much harder to see.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.maximum-subarray-kadane` — Maximum subarray (Kadane's algorithm)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.prefix-aggregate-recurrence` — The prefix-aggregate array as a DP recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-1.diagnose-empty-subarray-and-all-negative-boundary` — Diagnose the all-negative / empty-subarray boundary failure
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-1.counting-dp-over-linear-domain`

**Counting DP over a linear or rectangular domain, modulo a prime** · aliases: *counting DP*, *path counting mod p*

> What changes when a linear or grid DP counts rather than optimizes: the transition becomes a SUM over cases instead of a min/max, which is why disjointness stops being free and becomes a real obligation (a min over overlapping cases is harmless; a sum over them is wrong). And because the counts are astronomically large, the answers are reported modulo a prime, so every combine is modular — which is a genuine non-DP prerequisite and is drawn onto the registered anchor rather than assumed away. The node exists to make the optimization/counting distinction explicit rather than leaving it implicit in each family: it changes the proof obligation, not just the operator.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.grid-path-dp` — Grid / path DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.dp-correctness-proof-obligations` — What a DP recurrence must prove: the four obligations
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.modular-arithmetic` — Modular arithmetic (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.counting-vs-optimizing-objective`, `cl-3.formulate-digit-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E3`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the modulus is constitutive of this node (it draws anchor.modular-arithmetic), and the C++ one-liner it inherits is silently wrong in JavaScript. With the standard M = 1e9+7, two residues below M have a product up to ~1e18, which is ~111x past 2^53. So `(a * b) % 1000000007` — exact in C++ long long, and written without a thought — silently ROUNDS in JavaScript and returns a plausible, wrong residue. This is not a performance note: the direct translation of the C++ default produces wrong answers on most inputs. A correct JavaScript counting DP under a modulus requires BigInt for the multiply, or a split multiply keeping every partial product under 2^53 (decomposing one operand around 2^26, folding with Math.imul). Addition is safe (2M < 2^53); multiplication is not. This is the single highest-impact JavaScript effect in the map and it is invisible unless stated.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Retained pre-verdict mapper observation — SUB-8 (OUT-5) has SINCE delivered the verdict, recorded in the sibling `assessed: true` block above. This note is kept as the observation that foresaw the question, NOT as a claim that it is still open. Observation: modular multiplication of two values near a 1e9 prime exceeds JavaScript's exact-integer range for Number, so the C++ `long long` idiom every selected reference writes has no direct equivalent and forces a choice (BigInt, or splitting the multiply). It records that the question arises on this node's anchor edge.

### `cl-1.derive-recurrence-routine`

**Derive a state / transition / base case from a problem statement by routine**

> Execute the fixed derivation drill that turns a problem statement into a candidate recurrence, reliably and without inspiration: identify the last decision, determine what must be remembered once it is fixed, promote exactly that into the state, enumerate the transition as one case per decision option, identify the base cases, and name the state that holds the answer. What a learner acquires is REPEATABILITY — the ability to get a candidate recurrence out of any tractable statement without waiting for insight. The candidate may be wrong; that is what the proof and debugging nodes are for.

**Type** — `skill` / `procedural`

- `intra`   → `cl-1.judge-dp-applicability` — Judge whether a problem admits a DP formulation
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp`, `cl-1.formulate-edit-distance-recurrence`, `cl-1.prove-recurrence-correctness`, `cl-1.recognize-dp-formulation-under-unfamiliar-surface`

### `cl-1.diagnose-empty-subarray-and-all-negative-boundary`

**Diagnose the all-negative / empty-subarray boundary failure**

> Recognize the family of boundary failures created by an implicit empty-solution assumption: a max-subarray seeded at 0 returns 0 for an all-negative input, and the same defect recurs wherever a base case encodes "nothing selected" as an admissible answer that the problem does not admit. Includes the diagnostic habit that finds it — testing the degenerate input class (all-negative, single element, empty) rather than only the random one — and the fix (seed from the first element and let the recurrence, not the initializer, decide).

**Type** — `skill` / `debugging`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.maximum-subarray-kadane` — Maximum subarray (Kadane's algorithm)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.diagnose-wrong-state-transition-base-case` — Diagnose a wrong state, transition, or base case from a failing DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.dp-failure-modes` — The taxonomy of DP failure modes and their symptoms
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.circular-maximum-subarray`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E9`
- MATERIAL, and material in the direction that JavaScript REMOVES the C++ hazard — recorded because materiality is symmetric (JS-D5) and because the C++-derived version of this node teaches a bug that does not exist in JavaScript. The C++ idiom seeds the running maximum with INT_MIN or LLONG_MIN, and the classic failure this node exists to diagnose is that INT_MIN + a[i] UNDERFLOWS, so the sentinel is not a safe identity for max-plus. JavaScript's -Infinity is a TOTAL identity: -Infinity + x is -Infinity for any finite x, it compares correctly against every finite value, and it cannot underflow. So the all-negative boundary in JavaScript is handled by seeding -Infinity and nothing else goes wrong. A downstream agent inheriting the C++ failure taxonomy would teach a guard against a bug JavaScript cannot have, and would miss that the JavaScript sentinel is simply correct. The one JavaScript-specific residue: -Infinity is a Number, so an Int32Array cannot hold it — the sentinel forces a plain Array or Float64Array for the accumulator.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-1.diagnose-wrong-state-transition-base-case`

**Diagnose a wrong state, transition, or base case from a failing DP** · aliases: *DP fault localization*, *stress-testing a DP*

> Take a DP that produces a wrong answer and find which of the four obligations it violates, without rewriting it from scratch: shrink to the smallest failing case, compare the table against a brute force to find the FIRST divergent state (the one whose inputs are all correct — the defect is in that transition, not downstream of it), and read the defect class off the symptom. What a learner acquires is that a wrong DP is LOCALIZABLE rather than something to be stared at or replaced. The single highest-value skill in the cluster for a competitor, because the failure is common and the naive response — rewrite and hope — is unbounded.

**Type** — `skill` / `debugging`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.dp-failure-modes` — The taxonomy of DP failure modes and their symptoms
- `intra`   → `cl-1.dp-correctness-proof-obligations` — What a DP recurrence must prove: the four obligations

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.diagnose-empty-subarray-and-all-negative-boundary`

### `cl-1.difference-array-and-range-update`

**The difference array: the prefix recurrence run backwards** · aliases: *difference array*, *imos method*, *range update trick*

> The dual of the prefix array: store D[i] = a[i] - a[i-1], and a range update ("add v to every element of [l, r]") becomes two point writes, with the original array recovered by a prefix sweep. Why it belongs next to the prefix node rather than somewhere else: it is the SAME recurrence read in the other direction, and the pairing is the point — prefix turns point-updates plus range-queries into O(1) queries, difference turns range-updates plus a final read into O(1) updates. A learner who has one and not the other reaches for a segment tree on problems that do not need one.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.prefix-aggregate-recurrence` — The prefix-aggregate array as a DP recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-1.range-aggregate-by-difference` — Range aggregation by prefix difference (1D and 2D)

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-1.dp-correctness-proof-obligations`

**What a DP recurrence must prove: the four obligations**

> A DP is correct only if four separable things hold, and knowing they are four rather than one vague "it works" is the content of this node: (1) STATE SUFFICIENCY — the state captures everything the future depends on, so two histories reaching the same state are genuinely interchangeable; (2) TRANSITION EXHAUSTIVENESS — every way of reaching a state is covered by some case; (3) TRANSITION DISJOINTNESS — for counting DP, no way is covered twice (for optimization DP this weakens to "no case is invalid"); (4) BASE-CASE CORRECTNESS — the given values are right and the derived values never depend on an undefined one. Knowledge, not skill: these are the obligations, stated. Discharging them is cl-1.prove-recurrence-correctness.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-1.counting-dp-over-linear-domain`, `cl-1.diagnose-wrong-state-transition-base-case`, `cl-1.dp-failure-modes`, `cl-1.prove-path-transition-partition`, `cl-1.prove-recurrence-correctness`

### `cl-1.dp-failure-modes`

**The taxonomy of DP failure modes and their symptoms**

> The mapping from what a broken DP DOES to what is wrong with it, held as a taxonomy: an insufficient state (wrong answer on a case where two histories reaching the same state were not actually interchangeable); a non-exhaustive transition (answer too small on optimization, too low on counting); an overlapping transition (double-counted answer, exact-multiple-of-something in the classic case); a wrong or missing base case (wrong answer at the smallest input, often only there); an evaluation order that reads a not-yet-computed state (order- dependent garbage, frequently zero); an off-by-one at the domain boundary. The knowledge is the CORRESPONDENCE, not the list — the reason a symptom narrows the search is that different causes produce different symptoms.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.dp-correctness-proof-obligations` — What a DP recurrence must prove: the four obligations
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.diagnose-empty-subarray-and-all-negative-boundary`, `cl-1.diagnose-wrong-state-transition-base-case`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `1` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `2` |

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E1`, `JS-E2`, `JS-E9`
- MATERIAL — this node's content IS the symptom list, and JavaScript's symptom list differs from the C++ one this taxonomy inherits. Three concrete divergences a downstream agent must write: (1) JS-E1 — a too-deep DP dies at ~10^4 frames with 'RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded', a named, catchable, immediate failure; the C++ counterpart is a segfault an order of magnitude deeper, or silent stack corruption. (2) JS-E2 — arithmetic past 2^53 ROUNDS, producing a plausible near-miss; the C++ counterpart WRAPS, often producing an obviously absurd or negative value. A learner debugging in JavaScript therefore gets a weaker signal from a wrong number than a C++ learner does. (3) JS-E9 — reading an uncomputed state yields a spreading NaN (plain Array) or a silent, legitimate-looking 0 (typed array), where C++ yields UB. The taxonomy as inherited would under-describe all three.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Retained pre-verdict mapper observation — SUB-8 (OUT-5) has SINCE delivered the verdict, recorded in the sibling `assessed: true` block above. This note is kept as the observation that foresaw the question, NOT as a claim that it is still open. Observation: the "stack overflow" symptom that a C++-oriented reference attributes to deep top-down recursion presents differently in JavaScript (a RangeError at a non-tunable default depth reached at far smaller n), so the symptom→cause row for that failure mode may not transfer as written.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-1.edit-distance-dp`

**Edit distance DP (Levenshtein)** · aliases: *Levenshtein distance*, *edit distance*, *string edit DP*

> The canonical index-pair DP: dp[i][j] is the minimum number of insert / delete / replace operations converting the first i characters of A into the first j of B, with a transition that is a case analysis over the last operation and base cases along both empty-prefix edges. Its structural lesson, which outlives the problem: the state is a pair of INDEPENDENT prefixes and the transition moves the frontier by consuming from one string, the other, or both — the shape every alignment problem instantiates. CL-1 by D-F4 T4: a plain index tuple over a rectangular domain, difficulty in the recurrence.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.alignment-scoring-variants`, `cl-1.formulate-edit-distance-recurrence`, `cl-1.lcs-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp`

**Formulate a 1D sequence DP**

> Turn a sequence problem into a concrete dp[i]: decide what the index ranges over (elements consumed? position reached?), decide what the value means (the optimum over the prefix? the optimum ending at i?), write the transition as a case analysis on the decision at position i, and fix dp at the empty prefix. The recurring judgment this node carries: whether looking back ONE position suffices or the transition must look back further, and what that costs.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.select-linear-dp-pattern` — Choose which linear/sequence DP pattern fits a problem's shape
- `intra`   → `cl-1.derive-recurrence-routine` — Derive a state / transition / base case from a problem statement by routine
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.formulate-2d-sequence-dp`, `cl-1.formulate-kadane-best-ending-here`, `cl-1.formulate-lis-best-ending-here`, `cl-1.formulate-state-machine-over-sequence`, `cl-1.prove-prefix-state-sufficiency`, `cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic`, `cl-4.li-chao-tree-dp-application`, `cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp`, `cl-4.monotonic-queue-optimization`, `cl-4.recognize-envelope-structured-transition`, `cl-4.select-mainstream-optimization`

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — recorded here on the mapper's evidence, against this audit's own default that a formulation node is pre-code and therefore neutral. NEU-934's observation on this node is that a 1D DP over n up to 1e5-1e6 is THE FIRST PLACE a learner meets JavaScript's recursion ceiling, because the top-down form recurses once per index; the audit checked it and it holds. n = 1e5-1e6 against a cap on the order of 1e4 frames is not marginal, it is one to two orders of magnitude over, and 1D sequence DP is the most common shape in the map — so this is the highest-frequency instance of JS-E1, not the most exotic. The reason it lands on a FORMULATION node rather than an implementation one is a real gap, not a filing convenience: the node that owns the top-down/bottom-up realization choice is cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation, which is a FROZEN root (DR-S02) that this audit may not write, and which itself carries a mapper observation foreseeing exactly this finding. CL-1 has no unfrozen 1D-linear implementation node. Leaving this node neutral would therefore strand the map's most common recursion-depth hazard behind a frozen node — a silent C++ assumption of exactly the kind the second acceptance scenario forbids. The effect on what is written: in JavaScript the top-down/bottom-up choice is not the stylistic one the references present, because the bottom-up form is a loop and the top-down form does not run at this cluster's standard n. See JS-U1.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Retained pre-verdict mapper observation — SUB-8 (OUT-5) has SINCE delivered the verdict, recorded in the sibling `assessed: true` block above. This note is kept as the observation that foresaw the question, NOT as a claim that it is still open. Observation: a 1D DP over n up to 1e5-1e6 is the first place a learner meets JavaScript's recursion-depth ceiling, since the top-down form recurses once per index. The references assume a C++ default where the same formulation survives. It records where the issue first BITES in this cluster.

### `cl-1.formulate-2d-sequence-dp`

**Formulate a 2D sequence DP**

> Decide that a problem needs two indices and what each one ranges over — most commonly "the first i of A against the first j of B" — then write the transition as a case analysis over what happens to the two elements at the frontier (consume both, consume one, consume neither) and fix the base cases along both empty-prefix edges. The judgment carried: recognizing that a SECOND index is required at all, which is the same insufficiency signal the 1D sufficiency proof produces, met here in the shape that resolves it.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 1D sequence DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.select-linear-dp-pattern` — Choose which linear/sequence DP pattern fits a problem's shape
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-1.formulate-edit-distance-recurrence`, `cl-1.formulate-grid-path-dp`, `cl-1.formulate-sequence-partition-dp`, `cl-1.order-2d-table-evaluation`

### `cl-1.formulate-edit-distance-recurrence`

**Formulate the edit-distance recurrence**

> Derive the recurrence from the operation set: enumerate the last operation that could produce the (i, j) frontier, map each to the state it came from, and take the minimum, with the match case as the free transition. The judgment carried is that the operation set DEFINES the recurrence — drop replace and it becomes the LCS-complement, make the costs asymmetric and the case weights change — so the skill is deriving the recurrence FROM a cost model rather than recalling one recurrence.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.edit-distance-dp` — Edit distance DP (Levenshtein)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-2d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 2D sequence DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.derive-recurrence-routine` — Derive a state / transition / base case from a problem statement by routine
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.prove-edit-distance-case-exhaustiveness`

### `cl-1.formulate-kadane-best-ending-here`

**Formulate the maximum-subarray recurrence**

> Derive Kadane from the element-anchored pattern rather than recalling it: recognize that "the best subarray in the first i" cannot transition (it has forgotten whether it reaches i), anchor the endpoint instead, and read off the two-case transition. Paired deliberately with the LIS formulator — the same insufficiency, the same repair, two problems that look unrelated — because the pairing is what turns a trick into a transferable pattern.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.maximum-subarray-kadane` — Maximum subarray (Kadane's algorithm)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 1D sequence DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.prove-prefix-state-sufficiency` — Prove a prefix-index state is sufficient
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.prove-kadane-recurrence`

### `cl-1.formulate-lis-best-ending-here`

**Formulate LIS with an element-anchored state**

> Reject the prefix-optimum state for LIS and choose the element-anchored one, for the right reason: the transition needs to compare the candidate's last element against a[i], so the state must remember it, and anchoring the endpoint is what makes it available. What a learner acquires is not "the LIS recurrence" but the DISCRIMINATION — the ability to notice that a state has forgotten something the transition needs, before writing code that silently returns wrong answers.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.longest-increasing-subsequence-quadratic` — Longest increasing subsequence, O(n^2) recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 1D sequence DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.prove-prefix-state-sufficiency` — Prove a prefix-index state is sufficient
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.prove-lis-quadratic-recurrence`, `cl-1.transfer-lis-to-disguised-problems`

### `cl-1.formulate-state-machine-over-sequence`

**Formulate a sequence DP with an auxiliary mode dimension**

> Recognize that a constraint cannot be remembered by the index alone and promote exactly it into a mode dimension — no more, no less. The two failures the skill exists to prevent: too few modes (an insufficient state that silently returns wrong answers) and too many (modes that encode information the transition never reads, multiplying the state space for nothing). ### `cl-1.grid-path-dp`

**Grid / path DP** · aliases: *grid DP*, *path counting DP*, *lattice path DP*

> DP whose state is a cell coordinate on a rectangular grid and whose transition follows the permitted moves into that cell — counting paths, finding the cheapest path, or finding the best sub-rectangle anchored at a cell. What makes it distinct from a generic 2D sequence DP despite sharing the index-pair shape: the two indices are COUPLED by the movement rule rather than independent, so the transition is dictated by the geometry rather than chosen, and the dependency order follows the move directions. D-F4 places it in CL-1 by T4 — a plain index tuple over a rectangular domain. The restriction that keeps it here and out of CL-2: monotone moves only. A grid with unrestricted movement is a shortest-path problem on a graph, not a DP, because the state dependency stops being acyclic — that is the boundary, and it is the thing worth knowing about this family.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.counting-dp-over-linear-domain`, `cl-1.formulate-grid-path-dp`, `cl-3.implement-plug-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-1.handle-grid-boundaries-and-obstacles`

**Handle grid boundaries and obstacles**

> Make a grid DP correct at its edges: choose the sentinel that makes an out-of-bounds or blocked cell absorb correctly under THIS recurrence's operator, decide between a padded border and explicit bounds checks, and place the base case so it is not itself overwritten by the transition. Worth a node because the failure is universal and specific — a grid DP with a wrong boundary produces correct-looking output on the interior and is wrong only at the margin, which is the hardest kind of wrong to see.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-grid-path-dp` — Formulate a grid/path DP
- `intra`   → `cl-1.order-2d-table-evaluation` — Establish a valid evaluation order for a 2D table
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E9`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL — the guard idiom and the failure signature both differ from the C++ default. In C++ an out-of-range vector read is undefined behaviour, which is WHY the sentinel-padding idiom exists: the pad is not a convenience, it is what keeps the program defined. In JavaScript an out-of-range Array read is defined and returns undefined, which becomes NaN in arithmetic and then propagates through every downstream comparison silently (NaN < x is always false, so a max-fold quietly ignores it). So a JavaScript author CAN lean on the read returning undefined where a C++ author must not — but must then guard the NaN, a failure mode C++ does not have. JS-E5 compounds it: the same out-of-range read on an Int32Array is also undefined, but an uninitialized IN-range cell is 0, which for a path-counting grid is a legitimate value and therefore invisible. The correct JavaScript idiom (explicit bounds test, or sentinel padding chosen deliberately rather than inherited) must be written; it cannot be assumed from the C++ default.

### `cl-1.judge-dp-applicability`

**Judge whether a problem admits a DP formulation**

> Given a problem statement, judge whether BOTH DP properties hold together — that an optimal solution decomposes into subproblem optima AND that those subproblems recur — and therefore whether DP is the right tool at all. Distinct from the two roots it stands on, which each check ONE property in isolation: the acquisition here is the JOINT judgment, including the two ways it goes wrong (substructure without overlap, which is divide-and-conquer; overlap without substructure, which is a search problem memoization cannot rescue). This is the first thing a learner does with a problem and the first thing that silently fails: a learner who cannot make it writes a correct-looking DP for a problem that does not have one.

**Type** — `skill` / `conceptual`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-optimal-substructure` — Recognize optimal substructure in a problem  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-overlapping-subproblems` — Recognize overlapping subproblems and distinguish DP from divide-and-conquer  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.derive-recurrence-routine`, `cl-1.recognize-dp-formulation-under-unfamiliar-surface`, `cl-1.select-linear-dp-pattern`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-1.lcs-dp`

**Longest common subsequence DP** · aliases: *LCS*, *longest common subsequence*

> The alignment DP with a restricted operation set: dp[i][j] is the length of the longest subsequence common to the two prefixes, with a match extending the diagonal and a mismatch taking the better of dropping from either string. Mapped as its own node rather than folded into edit distance because it is the OTHER pole of the same shape — a maximization over matches rather than a minimization over operations — and because the pair is what teaches that the shape is independent of the objective. Also the bridge to LIS: the LCS of a sequence and its sorted deduplicated self is its LIS, a relationship worth holding.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.edit-distance-dp` — Edit distance DP (Levenshtein)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.alignment-scoring-variants`, `cl-2.palindromic-interval-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-1.linear-dp-pattern-catalogue`

**The catalogue of linear/sequence DP state patterns** · aliases: *DP state archetypes*, *linear DP patterns*

> The small set of recurring state shapes that covers most linear and rectangular DP, held as a comparable set rather than as isolated facts: prefix-index state ("answer over the first i"), element-anchored state ("best ending exactly at i"), two-sequence index pair ("first i of A against first j of B"), grid coordinate, sequence-plus-mode (a small auxiliary dimension), and index-plus-count (partition into k parts). What makes this a node rather than a list is the CONTRASTS between them — which shapes are interchangeable, which are not, and what question each one answers — because the strategic skill that stands on it is a choice, and a choice needs a compared set of alternatives, not an enumeration.

### `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d`

**1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)** · aliases: *1D DP*, *prefix DP*, *linear DP*

> The base shape of the whole cluster: a state indexed by a single position in a sequence, dp[i], whose value answers the problem restricted to the first i elements, and whose transition looks back a bounded distance. What makes it the simplest useful DP — the state space is linear, the dependency order is the index order itself (so evaluation order is free rather than a decision), and the transition is a small case analysis over what happens at position i. D-F4 assigns it here by T4, a confident residual: a plain index tuple over a linear domain, with no exotic encoding and no combinatorial structure to respect.

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

### `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d`

**2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)** · aliases: *2D DP*, *index-pair DP*, *two-sequence DP*

> A state indexed by a PAIR of positions, dp[i][j], where the two indices range over two independent linear domains — most often the prefixes of two sequences, sometimes a position paired with a resource count. What changes relative to 1D and why it is a separate node: the dependency order is no longer given for free by "increasing index", because a state can depend on neighbours in either dimension, so a valid evaluation order becomes something that must be ESTABLISHED rather than assumed. Still CL-1 by D-F4 T4: a plain index tuple over a rectangular domain, difficulty in the recurrence itself.

### `cl-1.longest-increasing-subsequence-quadratic`

**Longest increasing subsequence, O(n^2) recurrence** · aliases: *LIS*, *longest increasing subsequence*

> The canonical element-anchored DP: dp[i] is the length of the longest increasing subsequence ENDING EXACTLY at i, computed by scanning every earlier j with a[j] < a[i] and taking the best, with the answer the maximum over all i. Its importance to the cluster is out of proportion to the problem itself, because it is where the prefix-index state VISIBLY fails: "the LIS of the first i elements" is not a sufficient state, since extending a subsequence requires knowing what it ends with, and the prefix-optimum has forgotten. LIS is the standard worked example of the sufficiency obligation, which is why it sits on the shared element-anchored pattern node rather than owning that insight privately.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.best-ending-at-index-state-pattern` — Element-anchored state: "best ending exactly at i"
- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.formulate-lis-best-ending-here`, `cl-1.transfer-lis-to-disguised-problems`, `cl-2.recognize-an-implicit-dag`

*Notes (verbatim from the owning mapper):* SCOPE — THE CLUSTER LINE RUNS THROUGH THIS PROBLEM, BY DESIGN. This node is the O(n^2) RECURRENCE only. LIS in O(n log n) via a monotone structure is a DIFFERENT NODE IN A DIFFERENT CLUSTER (CL-4), and D-F4 §3.2 names this exact pair as its worked demonstration that same-problem/different-contribution splits across clusters: "LIS in O(n²) is CL-1; LIS in O(n log n) is CL-4. Same problem, different node." Recorded as EXC-5. Not a gap and not a contested assignment — the split is the partition working as specified, and `contested_by` is deliberately empty because CL-4 has no claim on the recurrence itself.

### `cl-1.max-product-subarray-two-value-state`

**Maximum product subarray: when one value per state is not enough** · aliases: *maximum product subarray*, *min-max paired state*

> Kadane's shape with multiplication instead of addition, and the reason it is a separate node rather than a footnote: the substitution BREAKS the state. A negative element turns the smallest product ending at i-1 into the largest ending at i, so the maximum alone is not a sufficient state and the DP must carry the running minimum ALONGSIDE the maximum. This is the cluster's clearest demonstration that state sufficiency depends on the OPERATOR and not only on the problem's shape — the same recurrence skeleton, one operator changed, and the state must grow. Worth its node for that lesson more than for the problem.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.maximum-subarray-kadane` — Maximum subarray (Kadane's algorithm)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.prove-prefix-state-sufficiency` — Prove a prefix-index state is sufficient
- `intra`   → `cl-1.best-ending-at-index-state-pattern` — Element-anchored state: "best ending exactly at i"

### `cl-1.maximum-subarray-kadane`

**Maximum subarray (Kadane's algorithm)** · aliases: *Kadane's algorithm*, *maximum subarray sum*, *max sum contiguous subarray*

> dp[i] is the maximum sum of a subarray ENDING EXACTLY at i, which is either a[i] alone or a[i] extending the best subarray ending at i-1 — whichever is larger — and the answer is the max over all i. Presented as a linear scan with a running variable, which is why it is usually taught as a trick and misfiled as greedy; mapped here as what it is, the minimal element-anchored DP, with the running variable a rolling window over a dp array that is never fully materialized. Understanding it AS a DP is the acquisition — a learner who has it as a trick cannot adapt it, and every one of its kin below is an adaptation.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.best-ending-at-index-state-pattern` — Element-anchored state: "best ending exactly at i"
- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.circular-maximum-subarray`, `cl-1.diagnose-empty-subarray-and-all-negative-boundary`, `cl-1.formulate-kadane-best-ending-here`, `cl-1.max-product-subarray-two-value-state`

### `cl-1.order-2d-table-evaluation`

**Establish a valid evaluation order for a 2D table**

> Determine an iteration order in which every state's dependencies are already computed when it is reached, and write the loops that realize it: row-major when the transition looks up and left, reverse order when it looks forward, and the recognition that some recurrences admit no simple nested-loop order and want the top-down form instead. What a learner acquires is that evaluation order is a CORRECTNESS obligation in 2D rather than a stylistic choice — the 1D case gave it away for free and thereby hid the lesson.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-2d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 2D sequence DP
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.handle-grid-boundaries-and-obstacles`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — this is the node that writes the 2D table, and JavaScript forces a decision C++ does not. The direct translation of the C++ default is an array-of-arrays, which in JavaScript is a boxed outer array of boxed inner arrays: a pointer chase per row plus per-element overhead. C++'s vector<vector<int>> is also row-indirect but the rows are unboxed contiguous ints, and the flat vector<int> is the reflexive idiom. The JavaScript idiom for a hot 2D table is therefore an explicit FLAT typed array with manual index arithmetic (i * W + j) — a choice a C++ author gets for free and a JavaScript author must make deliberately. JS-E6 is recorded here only in combination with that specific mitigable idiom, per JS-D1. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Retained pre-verdict mapper observation — SUB-8 (OUT-5) has SINCE delivered the verdict, recorded in the sibling `assessed: true` block above. This note is kept as the observation that foresaw the question, NOT as a claim that it is still open. Observation: allocating a 2D table in JavaScript has no single obvious form (array of arrays vs a flat typed array with manual index arithmetic), and the choice has materially different performance and initialization semantics than the C++ `vector<vector<int>>` or raw 2D array the references assume.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-1.prefix-aggregate-recurrence`

**The prefix-aggregate array as a DP recurrence** · aliases: *prefix sums*, *cumulative sums*, *partial sums*, *scan*

> S[i] = S[i-1] (+) a[i], with S[0] the identity — the smallest non-trivial DP in the map, and worth a node precisely because it is usually taught as an array trick rather than as what it is. Seeing it as a DP is load-bearing: it has a state (the prefix index), a transition (combine the previous prefix with the current element), a base case (the empty prefix maps to the operator's identity), and it earns its keep through overlapping subproblems exactly like every other DP here. It generalizes past sums to any associative operator with an identity — which is what makes prefix-xor, prefix-max-with-caveats, and prefix-count the same node rather than four tricks.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.build-prefix-aggregate`, `cl-1.circular-maximum-subarray`, `cl-1.difference-array-and-range-update`, `cl-1.range-aggregate-by-difference`, `cl-1.sequence-partition-dp`

It is NOT prefix-sum acceleration of another DP's transition, which answers T1 and is CL-4's — excluded here as EXC-2 rather than absorbed. ### `cl-1.prove-path-transition-partition`

**Prove a path transition partitions the solution set exactly once** · aliases: *last-move argument*, *no-double-count proof*

> Discharge the exhaustiveness and disjointness obligations for a counting DP over paths: argue that classifying paths by their LAST MOVE covers every path (there is always a last move, except at the base case) and covers none twice (a path has exactly one last move). The last-move classification is the standard instrument and the reason it works is worth acquiring explicitly — it is a partition of the solution set induced by a function on it, which is the general move that makes counting DP sound and that recurs in every counting family in the map.

**Type** — `skill` / `proof`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-grid-path-dp` — Formulate a grid/path DP

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-1.prove-prefix-state-sufficiency`

**Prove a prefix-index state is sufficient** · aliases: *state sufficiency argument*, *Markov property of a DP state*

> Establish the sufficiency obligation for a specific 1D formulation, which is the one that fails silently: a state that has forgotten something relevant still produces a table, still terminates, and still returns a plausible number. The skill is producing the interchangeability argument — and, when it cannot be produced, reading the failure as the signal that the state needs an extra dimension. That diagnostic direction is why this node is a prerequisite of the state-machine formulation below: a learner arrives at "this needs a mode dimension" by failing to prove sufficiency without one.

### `cl-1.prove-recurrence-correctness`

**Prove a DP recurrence correct and its optimal substructure sound** · aliases: *cut-and-paste argument*, *exchange argument for DP*

> Discharge the four obligations for a specific recurrence: argue state sufficiency (typically by cut-and-paste — assume a better solution through this state, splice it in, contradict optimality), argue the transition's cases are exhaustive and non-overlapping, argue the base cases, and argue the state dependency order is acyclic so the induction is well-founded. The general act; the per-technique proof nodes below are this skill met again where a specific recurrence makes one obligation hard.

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-1.prove-edit-distance-case-exhaustiveness`, `cl-1.prove-kadane-recurrence`, `cl-1.prove-lis-quadratic-recurrence`, `cl-1.prove-path-transition-partition`, `cl-1.prove-prefix-state-sufficiency`

### `cl-1.range-aggregate-by-difference`

**Range aggregation by prefix difference (1D and 2D)** · aliases: *range sum query*, *2D prefix sums*, *summed-area table*, *inclusion-exclusion on prefixes*

> What the prefix table is for: any range's aggregate is recoverable in O(1) from prefix values alone — S[r] - S[l-1] in one dimension, and the four-term inclusion-exclusion S[r][c] - S[r][c'-1] - S[r'-1][c] + S[r'-1][c'-1] in two. The requirement the identity rests on and that learners skip: the operator must be INVERTIBLE, which sums and xor are and max is not — the reason prefix-max does not answer range-max queries and why that question routes to a different structure entirely (anchor.segment-tree's territory, not this cluster's). Held as knowledge because the identity is a fact; applying it is folded into the formulators that depend on it rather than split into a node that would fail with them.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.prefix-aggregate-recurrence` — The prefix-aggregate array as a DP recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-1.build-prefix-aggregate` — Build a prefix-aggregate array

**Required by**

`cl-1.difference-array-and-range-update`, `cl-1.formulate-sequence-partition-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

**Recognize that an unfamiliar problem admits a known DP formulation**

> Strip a problem's surface story and see whether what remains is a shape this cluster already owns: that "minimum cost to make two configurations identical with insert/delete/replace-like operations" is an alignment DP however it is dressed, that a scheduling or chain-building constraint is an increasing- subsequence in disguise, that a linear scan with a reset condition is Kadane. consumer relying on it **must surface that reliance** (NEU-887 status discipline, inherited). The correct

flips it.***

*Notes (verbatim from the owning mapper):* TRANSFER-TO-DP UNCERTAINTY — CARRIED EXPLICITLY (NEU-887 R1; NEU-934 spec requires this node to carry it). This node asserts the skill EXISTS and belongs in the map. It does NOT assert that the skill is reliably teachable, that practising the cluster's other nodes produces it, or that it transfers from mapped techniques to unmapped surfaces. NEU-887 R1 (the DP-transfer gap) says the opposite is a live possibility: transfer is the DP skill most often assumed and least often evidenced. NEU-932 F-C-5 sharpens the point — no selected corpus is ordered by LEARNING dependency, and C2's authored ramp is one expert's design (class-1 evidence about a design, not about learning), so the prerequisite edges drawn into this node are a STRUCTURAL claim about what the skill presupposes, NOT an empirical claim that acquiring them yields it. Consequence for downstream consumers, stated so it is not rediscovered: a curriculum that teaches this cluster's nodes and expects transfer to follow is relying on an unevidenced step. The uncertainty is not resolvable inside NEU-889 — no evidence class available to this charter could settle it (it would need learner-outcome data, class 5/6, which NEU-887 records as absent). Recorded, owned, and left open rather than smoothed. Node stays `provisional`; `evidence_class: 1` describes the node's EXISTENCE claim (the references name the skill), not its acquisition claim, which has none. Same uncertainty applies undiminished to cl-1.transfer-lis-to-disguised-problems.

### `cl-1.reconstruct-optimal-solution-by-backtracking`

**Reconstruct an optimal solution by backtracking the table**

> Walk a completed table backwards from the answer state to a base case, emitting the decision taken at each step, to produce the actual alignment / path / subsequence / partition rather than just its score. Includes the two boundary cases that break naive traceback: ties (several predecessors achieve the value — any one is a valid optimum, and picking deterministically matters when the judge demands a specific one) and the base-case stop condition.

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.solution-reconstruction` — Solution reconstruction: the table holds more than the answer
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

> Read a problem's shape and pick the state pattern that fits it: recognize that "count the ways to reach the end" wants a prefix-index state while "longest run ending somewhere" wants an element-anchored one, that two independent sequences want an index pair, and that a constraint the prefix index cannot remember wants an extra mode dimension rather than a cleverer transition. ### `cl-1.sequence-partition-dp`

**Sequence partition DP (index-plus-count state)** · aliases: *array partition DP*, *split into k segments*, *segmented least squares shape*

> The shape for "split a sequence into k contiguous pieces, optimally": dp[i][j] = the best way to split the first i elements into j pieces, with a transition that ranges over where the last piece starts. Its two distinguishing properties: the second index is a COUNT rather than a position, and the transition is a linear scan rather than a bounded look-back — which makes the naive evaluation O(n^2 k) and makes this family the standard base for the divide-and-conquer and Knuth optimizations. Those optimizations are NOT this node and NOT this cluster (D-F4 T1 → CL-4; recorded as EXC-4). The base recurrence is CL-1 by T4: the domain is linear, the state is a plain tuple, and the difficulty is the recurrence itself.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.prefix-aggregate-recurrence` — The prefix-aggregate array as a DP recurrence
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.formulate-sequence-partition-dp`, `cl-4.aliens-trick-application`, `cl-4.divide-and-conquer-optimization`, `cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-dp`, `cl-4.smawk-application`

### `cl-1.solution-reconstruction`

**Solution reconstruction: the table holds more than the answer** · aliases: *path recovery*, *traceback*, *argmax recovery*

> A completed DP table does not merely hold the optimal VALUE — it holds enough information to recover an optimal SOLUTION, because the value at each state was achieved by some predecessor and that predecessor is identifiable after the fact. The two ways to exploit this and what each costs: store a parent pointer per state during evaluation (memory proportional to the state space, trivial recovery), or store nothing and recompute at recovery time which predecessor achieves the value (no extra memory, a second pass). Also the fact that makes reconstruction non-obvious: the answer is a PATH through the state space, not a state, so "the DP is correct" and "I can produce the thing it is optimal over" are different claims.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.reconstruct-optimal-solution-by-backtracking`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-1.state-machine-over-sequence`

**Sequence DP with an auxiliary mode dimension (state-machine DP)** · aliases: *state-machine DP*, *DP with a mode dimension*, *automaton-free multi-state DP*

> The shape that appears when a prefix index alone is not a sufficient state: dp[i] becomes dp[i][m] where m ranges over a SMALL, FIXED set of modes the process can be in — holding or not holding an asset, in a cooldown, having used k of a resource, having taken or skipped element i-1. The transition becomes a small transition matrix between modes. Why it stays in CL-1 rather than moving to CL-3: the mode is a plain index in a tuple, not an encoding — nothing is packed, compressed, or reinterpreted (D-F4 T2 answers no), and the domain is still linear (T3 no), so T4 catches it. The family covers the non-adjacent-selection and buy/sell-with-constraints problems that are the cluster's most common exam material.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)
- `intra`   → `cl-1.linear-dp-pattern-catalogue` — The catalogue of linear/sequence DP state patterns
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-1.alignment-scoring-variants`, `cl-1.formulate-state-machine-over-sequence`

"State-machine DP" is a community name, not a taxonomy term, and it collides conceptually with CL-3's AUTOMATON DP, which is a different thing (there the state IS an automaton node built from the input — an encoding, D-F4 T2; here the mode set is small, fixed, and read straight off the problem statement). The two are not the same technique and this node makes no claim on CL-3's. Aliases carried so the audit does not read the name overlap either as a duplicate or as a coverage gap.

### `cl-1.transfer-lis-to-disguised-problems`

**Recognize an increasing-subsequence structure under a disguise** · aliases: *box stacking*, *chain of pairs*, *nesting problems*

> See the LIS under the costume: recognize that "how many boxes can nest", "the longest chain of pairs", and "the largest set of mutually compatible items under a two-dimensional order" are the same problem, and — the harder half — recognize what the disguise ADDS, since two-dimensional variants require a sort that reduces one dimension away before the LIS applies, and the tie-breaking rule in that sort is where the reduction is usually got wrong.

**Type** — `skill` / `transfer`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-1.longest-increasing-subsequence-quadratic` — Longest increasing subsequence, O(n^2) recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-1.formulate-lis-best-ending-here` — Formulate LIS with an element-anchored state
- `intra`   → `cl-1.recognize-dp-formulation-under-unfamiliar-surface` — Recognize that an unfamiliar problem admits a known DP formulation
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-optimal-substructure` — Recognize optimal substructure in a problem  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

## CL-2 — Combinatorial / structural

*DP whose state is indexed over a nontrivial combinatorial structure — the structure, not the encoding, is what makes it hard.*

**61 nodes** · files: `nodes/cl-2-combinatorial.yaml`

### `cl-2.augment-an-insufficient-interval-state`

**Augment an interval state that is not sufficient**

> Detect that endpoints alone under-determine a range's answer, and repair the formulation by carrying the minimum additional context — no more, because every axis multiplies the table. The judgment is the whole skill: the naive repair (carry everything relevant) is always correct and usually intractable, and the acquisition is finding the smallest sufficient augmentation.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.interval-state-with-boundary-context` — Interval state augmented with boundary context
- `intra`   → `cl-2.prove-interval-dp-correctness` — Prove an interval DP correct by induction on span
- `intra`   → `cl-2.formulate-interval-dp` — Formulate an interval DP: choose the endpoints and the joining cost
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-2.bounded-knapsack-multiplicity`

**Bounded knapsack: per-item multiplicity limits** · aliases: *multiple knapsack*, *k-bounded knapsack*

> The variant in which item i may be taken up to k_i times, and its naive correct recurrence: the transition enumerates how many copies are taken. The point a learner acquires is that bounded knapsack is neither of the other two — it degenerates to 0/1 at k=1 and to unbounded as k grows past the capacity, and in between the multiplicity is a real third structure that neither loop direction expresses.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.zero-one-knapsack-recurrence` — 0/1 knapsack recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.unbounded-knapsack-recurrence` — Unbounded knapsack recurrence
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.select-knapsack-variant`, `cl-4.monotonic-queue-optimization`

### `cl-2.child-aggregation`

**Child aggregation** · aliases: *combining children*, *merge over children*

> That a tree DP's transition is a fold over an arbitrary-arity child list rather than a fixed-arity expression, and what follows: the combining operation must be associative and commutative in the child order (the children have no order), and the identity of that fold is the leaf base case. Recognizing the fold is what lets a learner write the transition once for binary and general trees alike.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.subtree-indexed-state-space` — Subtree-indexed state space

### `cl-2.combination-vs-permutation-loop-order`

**Loop nesting decides whether a counting DP counts combinations or permutations** · aliases: *ordered vs unordered coin change*, *counting each subset once*

> In an unbounded counting DP, putting the item loop outside the amount loop counts each multiset once (combinations); putting the amount loop outside counts every ordering of the same multiset (permutations/sequences). The two loops are the same three lines in a different order and neither errors. The underlying fact is that the outer loop is what imposes a canonical order on the choices, and imposing one is exactly what makes each combination counted once.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.counting-vs-optimizing-objective` — Counting versus optimizing over one state space
- `intra`   → `cl-2.unbounded-knapsack-recurrence` — Unbounded knapsack recurrence

**Required by**

`cl-2.implement-counting-dp-loop-nesting`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-2.condense-sccs-to-recover-a-dag`

**Condense strongly connected components to recover a DAG** · aliases: *SCC condensation*, *condensation DAG*, *component graph DP*

> When the input graph is cyclic, change the state: collapse each strongly connected component to a single vertex, observe that the condensation is by construction a DAG, and run the DP on it — with each component's own internal contribution folded into its vertex weight. The judgment a learner acquires is when this is legitimate: it is exactly when the answer does not distinguish vertices within a component, and it is wrong when it does.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.cyclic-state-dependency-breaks-dp` — A cyclic state dependency breaks the recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.dp-on-an-explicit-dag` — DP on an explicit DAG

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

The audit's initial verdict was neutral on the ground that the strategic act (recognizing the cycles are genuine and that condensing recovers a DAG) is language-independent, and that the SCC computation is a boundary anchor whose internals DR-S03 forbids decomposing. The mapper's observation on this node reaches further and is right: the strategic act COMMITS the learner to running an SCC algorithm, and BOTH standard ones — Tarjan and Kosaraju — are recursive DFS in their C++ default form, so at the 2e5-vertex DAGs this technique is reached for they descend far past JavaScript's ~1e4 frame cap and do not run. That is not an internal detail of the anchor, it is the feasibility of the step this node tells the learner to take, and a node that says 'condense the SCCs' while the only realizations it implies crash is a silent C++ assumption. The JavaScript-feasible forms are an iterative Tarjan with an explicit stack, or Kosaraju with both passes iterative. SCOPE CAVEAT PRESERVED, not smoothed: the anchor's own internals remain out of this audit's reach per DR-S03, so this verdict is recorded on the DP-side act that depends on them rather than on the anchor, and the anchor-side question stays open as JS-U4.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only, NOT a verdict — OUT-5 (SUB-8) owns it. Standard SCC algorithms (Tarjan, Kosaraju) are recursive, so the recursion-depth exposure recorded on cl-2.debug-tree-dp-recursion-depth applies to this node's prerequisite step too. Recorded for SUB-8; not a verdict.

### `cl-2.counting-vs-optimizing-objective`

**Counting versus optimizing over one state space** · aliases: *semiring view of DP*, *count-ways vs optimize*

> That the state space and the transition graph of a counting DP and an optimizing DP can be identical and only the AGGREGATION differs — sum over incoming transitions instead of min/max — and what changes as a consequence: base cases become 1 rather than 0 or infinity, unreachable states become 0 rather than a sentinel, and every transition must now be counted exactly once rather than merely considered. The last clause is where counting DP gets hard, and it is what the loop-order node below is about.

### `cl-2.cyclic-state-dependency-breaks-dp`

**A cyclic state dependency breaks the recurrence** · aliases: *cyclic dependency*, *ill-founded recurrence*, *functional-graph cycle*

> That a state graph with a cycle has no well-founded recurrence — the values are not defined by the transitions, they are only constrained by them — and that this is a property of the FORMULATION rather than of the problem. A cycle means the state was chosen wrongly, or that the problem is a system of equations (expectation DP over a cyclic chain) or a shortest-path problem rather than a DP. The failure mode a learner acquires: a cycle in the states is not a bug to patch but a signal that the formulation must change.

- `intra`   → `cl-2.dp-state-graph-is-a-dag` — Every DP's state graph is a DAG
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.condense-sccs-to-recover-a-dag`, `cl-2.debug-cyclic-state-dependency`, `cl-2.recognize-an-implicit-dag`

### `cl-2.debug-cyclic-state-dependency`

**Diagnose a memoized DP that never terminates**

> Distinguish "the recursion is too deep" from "the recursion is circular" — the same crash, opposite fixes. The discriminator a learner acquires is an in-progress marker in the memo: a state re-entered while its computation is unfinished proves a cycle, whereas a legitimately deep chain never re-enters. Having proved the cycle, the repair is a reformulation (a new state, or a condensation), never a bigger stack.

**Type** — `skill` / `debugging`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.cyclic-state-dependency-breaks-dp` — A cyclic state dependency breaks the recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.impose-topological-evaluation-order` — Impose a topological evaluation order on a DAG DP
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| Dimension | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |
| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `3` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `4` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-4` |
| `proof_obligation_load` | `0` |
| `recognition_load` | `3` |
| `state_formulation_load` | `1` |
| `transition_derivation_load` | `1` |

*`creator_review: "deferred-provisional"` — the creator plausibility review did NOT run (charter
Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL — and the finding is that JavaScript makes this diagnosis AMBIGUOUS in a way C++ does not. The node's premise is a memoized DP that never terminates. In C++ an infinite memoized recursion consumes the stack for a long time and then segfaults at a depth on the order of 1e5-1e6, which is far beyond any legitimate DP depth, so the failure itself nearly identifies the cause. In JavaScript it throws RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded at a depth on the order of 1e4 — WHICH IS EXACTLY THE SAME ERROR, AT THE SAME THRESHOLD, THAT A CORRECT-BUT-DEEP TREE OR DAG DP THROWS (JS-E1, cl-2.debug-tree-dp-recursion-depth). So in JavaScript the symptom does not distinguish 'my state graph has a cycle' from 'my state graph is fine and 2e5 deep', and the two have completely different fixes: find the cycle, versus convert to an explicit stack. The learner must disambiguate by other means — instrumenting the recursion path, or checking whether the depth grows with input size. That extra diagnostic step is JavaScript-specific and a C++-derived version of this node does not contain it. Additionally the premise itself shifts: 'never terminates' is a C++ description; in JavaScript it terminates, quickly, with an error.

### `cl-2.debug-interval-order-violation`

**Diagnose an interval DP that reads uncomputed states**

> Recognize the specific signature of an order violation — the table is fully populated, no index is out of bounds, nothing crashes, and the answer is silently built on unfinished states — and localize it by comparing against a memoized version that cannot violate the order. The technique a learner acquires is the differential: an order bug is exactly the bug that top-down cannot have.

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E9`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL — the symptom this node diagnoses has a container-dependent signature in JavaScript with no routine C++ counterpart. The bug is reading a state not yet computed. In C++ the table is typically a zero-initialized vector, so the read returns 0 and the wrong answer is a too-small optimum — usually one signature. In JavaScript it DEPENDS ON THE CONTAINER CHOICE (JS-E5), which the author made for performance reasons and now discovers has changed their debugging: a plain Array yields undefined, so the arithmetic yields NaN and the wrongness SPREADS and is loud, visible immediately and traceable to the first bad read; an Int32Array yields a silent 0, indistinguishable from a legitimately computed zero, so the wrongness is quiet and looks like a modelling error rather than an ordering error. A downstream agent must teach both signatures and must teach that the container decides which one the learner will see. That is a JavaScript-specific differential the inherited C++ taxonomy does not contain.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-2.debug-item-reuse-from-loop-direction`

**Diagnose item reuse or omission from the capacity-loop direction**

> Given a knapsack that produces a plausible but wrong number, isolate the cause: reconstruct which items the table thinks it took, notice a multiplicity that the problem forbids (or a reuse the problem allows and the table refused), and trace it to the read/write ordering of the collapsed array. This is a genuinely separate acquisition from writing the loop: a learner who can write it correctly often cannot yet read a wrong answer back to it, because the code looks right.
