### `cl-2.debug-tree-dp-recursion-depth`

**Diagnose a tree DP that dies on a path-shaped tree**

> Recognize the shape of the failure — passes every sample, dies only at full n, and the failing tree is degenerate — and repair it by converting the traversal to an explicit stack or by processing vertices in a precomputed reverse-BFS order, which needs no recursion at all. What a learner acquires is that the worst case of a tree is a path, and that "trees are shallow" is an assumption their code makes silently.

**Type** — `skill` / `debugging`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs` — Implement a tree DP as a post-order DFS
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| Dimension | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |
| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `3` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `5` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-4` |
| `proof_obligation_load` | `0` |
| `recognition_load` | `3` |
| `state_formulation_load` | `1` |
| `transition_derivation_load` | `1` |

*`creator_review: "deferred-provisional"` — the creator plausibility review did NOT run (charter
Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — this node IS the recursion-depth node, and both its threshold and its signature are language-specific. It diagnoses a tree DP that dies on a path-shaped tree. In C++ that death is a SEGFAULT (or a silent stack smash) at a depth that is platform-dependent (often ~1e5-1e6), and raising the stack limit is an option that may exist. In JavaScript it is a NAMED, CATCHABLE RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded at a depth on the order of 1e4, and raising the limit IS NOT AN OPTION for a submitted program, so the only fix is the explicit-stack rewrite (cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs). Three things a downstream agent must therefore write differently: the threshold is ~10x lower, the diagnosis is easier (a named error naming its own cause, versus a segfault that names nothing), and the remedy set is strictly smaller (one option, not two). The node's C++-derived form would teach a fix JavaScript does not have.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only, NOT a verdict — OUT-5 (SUB-8) owns it. Recorded because this node is where the root-level observation on cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation (recursion depth is language-material in JavaScript) becomes acute rather than theoretical: a path-shaped tree of 2*10^5 vertices is a routine constraint and is far beyond JavaScript's default stack, which — unlike the C++ that every selected reference assumes — has no standard tunable equivalent. The explicit-stack rewrite may therefore be mandatory in JavaScript where it is optional in the references. SUB-8 must reach its own verdict; this note is not one.

### `cl-2.descending-vs-ascending-capacity-loop`

**Capacity-loop direction determines item multiplicity**

> When the two-axis table is collapsed to a single capacity array, the direction of the capacity loop decides WHICH RECURRENCE is computed: descending reads only values from the previous item's row and computes 0/1; ascending reads values already updated for the current item and computes unbounded. The direction is not a detail of the same DP — it is the selector between two different DPs that share one line of code.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.zero-one-knapsack-recurrence` — 0/1 knapsack recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.unbounded-knapsack-recurrence` — Unbounded knapsack recurrence
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.implement-knapsack-in-place-capacity-loop`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

A T1 reading says: the 1D collapse takes a correct 2D recurrence and reduces its memory, therefore CL-4 (optimization) owns it. T1 is rejected here on T1's OWN wording — it requires the reduction leave "what the states MEAN" unchanged, and the ascending loop changes the recurrence from 0/1 to unbounded. A transformation that selects between two different semantics is not a cost reduction of one of them. The pure space-saving reading (descending only, semantics fixed) IS a live CL-4 claim and is carried, not smoothed. Route: D-F4a challenge (U4).

### `cl-2.dp-on-an-explicit-dag`

**DP on an explicit DAG** · aliases: *DAG DP*, *DP over a given graph*

> The family in which the DAG is not implied by the recurrence but GIVEN in the input — vertices are the states, the input's edges are the transitions, and the DP's value is defined per vertex. What is new relative to every earlier family is that the state space is arbitrary rather than derived, so nothing about the input suggests an evaluation order and one must be computed rather than read off a loop.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.dp-state-graph-is-a-dag` — Every DP's state graph is a DAG

### `cl-2.dp-state-graph-is-a-dag`

**Every DP's state graph is a DAG** · aliases: *subproblem graph*, *state graph*, *DP as DAG traversal*

> The unifying fact of the whole map: a DP's states are vertices, its transitions are edges, and the requirement that the recurrence be well-founded is exactly the requirement that this graph be acyclic. Every family in every cluster is DP over a DAG — the item prefix, the interval containment order, and the rooted tree are all DAGs. What a learner acquires is the retro-reading that makes the evaluation-order problem one problem rather than one per family.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.cyclic-state-dependency-breaks-dp`, `cl-2.dp-on-an-explicit-dag`, `cl-2.recognize-an-implicit-dag`

### `cl-2.exclude-one-child-aggregate`

**Excluding one child from an aggregate** · aliases: *prefix-suffix child aggregates*, *leave-one-out aggregate*, *invertible merge*

> The technical core of rerooting, and the part that is not the idea: to pass a value down to child c, a vertex must aggregate all its children EXCEPT c, and doing that naively per child is quadratic in the degree — which on a star is quadratic overall and defeats the whole point. The two repairs are prefix-and-suffix aggregates over the child list (always available), or subtracting c's contribution when the aggregate is invertible (available for sums, not for maxima). Knowing WHICH applies is knowing whether the fold has an inverse, and that is the fact this node carries.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.rerooting-principle` — Rerooting principle
- `intra`   → `cl-2.child-aggregation` — Child aggregation

**Required by**

`cl-2.implement-rerooting-two-pass-dfs`, `cl-2.prove-rerooting-correctness`, `cl-2.recognize-rerooting-applicability`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| `implementation_load` | `2` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `5` |

T1 requires an already-correct recurrence whose cost is reduced. The exclude-one-child aggregate is not an acceleration of rerooting — it is a CONSTITUENT of it: without it the up-value cannot be computed at all in the invertible-free case, because the naive per-child recomputation is the thing rerooting exists to avoid. A technique whose absence leaves no correct recurrence is not an optimization of one.

### `cl-2.formulate-subtree-recurrence`

**Formulate a subtree recurrence: decide what a subtree must summarize**

> Decide the contract between a subtree and its parent: what a child must report so that the parent can decide without looking inside it. ### `cl-2.formulate-zero-one-knapsack`

**Formulate a 0/1 knapsack: choose the resource axis and the item order**

> Given a selection problem with one or more bounded resources, decide what the DP indexes: which quantity is the capacity axis (small, integral, bounded) and which is maximized (possibly large, possibly real). The canonical exercise is the knapsack whose weights are huge and whose values are small — where the naive axis choice is intractable and the swapped one is not. A learner acquires the judgment, not the table.

- `cross`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d` — 1D linear/sequence DP (prefix-index state)  *(CL-2→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-2.formulate-zero-one-knapsack->cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-1d`)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

The blind prediction's id was WRONG and the to_name was close — which is exactly why schema §4.3 makes `to_name` SUB-12's resolution key rather than `to_node`. Still DECLARED, not drawn: SUB-12 owns realization regardless of whether the target happens to be visible now.

### `cl-2.implement-counting-dp-under-a-modulus`

**Implement a counting DP under a modulus**

> Keep a counting DP's values in range throughout: reduce at the right points, handle the negative results of subtractive transitions, and know that reduction hides overflow rather than preventing it. *nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E3`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the highest-impact single finding in this cluster. This is the node that writes the modular arithmetic, and the C++ idiom it inherits is silently wrong in JavaScript. With M = 1e9+7 (the modulus essentially every counting problem uses), two residues below M multiply to ~1e18, which is ~111x past 2^53. C++'s `(a * b) % M` in long long is exact and is written reflexively. JavaScript's identical line silently ROUNDS and returns a plausible wrong residue — it does not throw, does not wrap to a negative, and does not announce itself. The addition folds are fine (2M is far below 2^53); it is multiplication alone that breaks, which is why the bug survives casual testing on small inputs where products stay under 2^53. Correct JavaScript requires either BigInt for the multiply-and-reduce (correct, and materially slower — JS-E3), or a split multiply that decomposes one operand around 2^26 so no partial product leaves the exact range (Math.imul-based). A downstream agent authoring this node in JavaScript MUST write one of them; inheriting the C++ default produces a wrong solution that looks right.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only, NOT a verdict — OUT-5 (SUB-8) owns it. Recorded because it is the sharpest JavaScript divergence SUB-4 met: every selected reference assumes a 64-bit integer type in which a product of two values below 10^9 is exact. JavaScript's Number is not that type, so the standard "multiply then reduce" step is unsound above 2^53 and needs BigInt or a split-multiply. This is a language-level break in a step the references treat as trivial. SUB-8 must reach its own verdict; this note is not one.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

- MATERIAL — this is the node that writes the n^2 table and the O(n^3) loop nest, and the container decision is JavaScript's to make. C++'s vector<vector<int>> is also a row-of-rows, not one contiguous table, though its rows hold unboxed ints; the JavaScript translation can additionally box the elements, read in the innermost loop of a cubic algorithm — a bad place to pay a pointer chase and a possible unboxing. The JavaScript idiom is a flat Int32Array with i * n + j addressing. Recorded here rather than on cl-2.interval-evaluation-order-by-length because the order is a dependency fact and the table is a written artifact (JS-D2). Deliberate non-claim on JS-E1: the length-major loop is ITERATIVE, so it is exactly the realization that sidesteps the recursion cap — which is worth stating, because the memoized top-down alternative a C++ author might reach for instead does not sidestep it. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).

### `cl-2.implement-knapsack-in-place-capacity-loop`

**Implement a knapsack over a single in-place capacity array**

> Write the collapsed loop correctly for the chosen variant: pick the direction from the recurrence's read/write dependency rather than from memory, set the array's initial values to encode the base case (zero-initialised for "any capacity may be unfilled", minus-infinity or a sentinel for "exact capacity required"), and keep the bound arithmetic from underflowing. The base-case initialisation is a second, independent failure point that a learner meets here and nowhere else.

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — this is the canonical performance-bound DP table in the map, and it is the node that writes it. The C++ default is vector<int> dp(W+1): contiguous, unboxed, 4 bytes a cell, chosen without deliberation. The direct JavaScript translation, new Array(W+1).fill(0), is a boxed polymorphic object whose packed-SMI representation is an engine optimization rather than a guarantee, and is lost on a single non-SMI write. The JavaScript idiom that matches the C++ default's actual behaviour is an explicit Int32Array(W+1) — a decision the C++ author never makes. The in-place single-array form makes this sharper, not softer: the whole technique exists to collapse the table to one row for memory, so the per-element overhead of the container is exactly what the technique is optimizing, and choosing the wrong container gives the memory back. NEU-935's observation on this node adds the interaction the audit confirms and that makes the choice non-obvious: THE SENTINEL CONSTRAINS THE CONTAINER. A knapsack that must distinguish 'unreachable' from 'reachable with value 0' seeds -Infinity, and -Infinity CANNOT LIVE IN AN Int32Array — it coerces to 0, silently turning every unreachable state into a legitimate-looking zero. So the typed-array choice forces either a Float64Array (which keeps -Infinity but doubles the memory the technique was collapsing the table to save) or an explicit integer sentinel with its own guard. C++'s single number type does not exist; its int and its INT_MIN are the same width and the question never arises. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only, NOT a verdict — OUT-5 (SUB-8) owns the verdict. A typed-array (Int32Array/Float64Array) capacity row behaves very differently from a plain JS array here, and the sentinel choice interacts with JavaScript's single number type in a way the C++ that every selected reference assumes does not surface. Recorded for SUB-8.

### `cl-2.implement-rerooting-two-pass-dfs`

**Implement rerooting as two traversals**

> Write the two passes: a post-order pass computing every down-value, then a pre-order pass carrying each vertex's up-value to its children, combining the parent's up-value with the parent's other children's down-values. The per-vertex answer is the fold of its down-values with its up-value. The implementation risk is that both passes look plausible and only the up-value can be wrong.

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.recognize-rerooting-applicability` — Recognize when rerooting applies and when the aggregate forbids it
- `intra`   → `cl-2.exclude-one-child-aggregate` — Excluding one child from an aggregate
- `intra`   → `cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs` — Implement a tree DP as a post-order DFS
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-2.debug-rerooting-self-inclusion`

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — rerooting is TWO traversals of the same tree, so it meets JS-E1 twice, and the second pass is the harder conversion. The upward (post-order) pass has the same problem as any tree DP: depth 2e5 on a path-shaped tree against a ~1e4 JavaScript cap, no raisable limit. The downward pass is worse: it carries a value from parent to child, so the explicit-stack rewrite must push the parent's contribution onto the stack alongside the vertex, whereas the recursion carried it implicitly in the frame as an argument. The C++ default writes both passes as plain recursion and never confronts this. In JavaScript both passes must be rewritten with explicit stacks, and the downward one's per-frame payload must be made explicit — a genuine acquisition, at exactly the tree sizes rerooting is FOR (rerooting exists to avoid the O(n^2) of re-running the DP from every root, so it is only reached for when n is large — precisely where the JavaScript stack fails).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only, NOT a verdict — OUT-5 (SUB-8) owns it. Rerooting is two full traversals, so the recursion-depth exposure noted on cl-2.debug-tree-dp-recursion-depth applies twice over. Recorded for SUB-8; this note is not a verdict.

### `cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs`

**Implement a tree DP as a post-order DFS**

> Realize the recurrence: recurse into each child, fold the returned values into the accumulator, apply the vertex's own contribution, return. The base case is not written separately — it is what the fold produces on the empty child list, and seeing that is part of the acquisition.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.formulate-subtree-recurrence` — Formulate a subtree recurrence: decide what a subtree must summarize
- `intra`   → `cl-2.root-an-unrooted-tree` — Root an unrooted tree
- `intra`   → `cl-2.child-aggregation` — Child aggregation

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the flagship recursion finding of this audit. This node writes the post-order DFS, and the post-order DFS is exactly the shape JavaScript cannot run at competitive sizes. C++'s recursive post-order on a tree of n = 2e5 reaches depth 2e5 on a path-shaped tree; a typical 8 MB stack absorbs it and some judges raise the limit further, platform-dependent. JavaScript throws RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded at a depth on the order of 1e4 — an order of magnitude BELOW the input sizes this technique routinely meets — and a submitted program cannot raise the limit. So the recursive realization is not slow in JavaScript, it does not run at all on the standard adversarial input. The JavaScript realization must convert to an EXPLICIT STACK, which is materially harder than the recursion it replaces: post-order specifically requires either a two-phase visit marker or an iterator-position-per-node scheme, because a child's value must be folded into the parent AFTER all children are done. That conversion is a real acquisition a C++ learner never makes. A downstream agent that inherits the C++ default here ships an exercise that crashes.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

them; the withdrawals are upheld.*

### `cl-2.impose-topological-evaluation-order`

**Impose a topological evaluation order on a DAG DP** · aliases: *topological sort DP*, *processing states in topological order*

> Give a DAG DP a valid evaluation order by computing a topological order of the state graph and processing states along it — or, equivalently, by memoizing and letting the recursion discover the order. What a learner acquires is that the two are the same act: topological order is what memoization computes implicitly, which is why top-down needs no ordering step and bottom-up does.

**Type** — `skill` / `procedural`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.dp-on-an-explicit-dag` — DP on an explicit DAG
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.counting-paths-in-a-dag`, `cl-2.debug-cyclic-state-dependency`, `cl-2.longest-path-in-a-dag`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING, and it changes WHICH ALGORITHM is chosen — the sharpest form the recursion cap takes. There are two standard ways to impose a topological order: a recursive DFS with post-order emission, and Kahn's iterative in-degree BFS. In C++ they are interchangeable and the DFS one is often preferred for brevity. In JavaScript they are NOT interchangeable: the DFS recurses to the DAG's longest path, which on a 2e5-vertex DAG is up to 2e5 frames against a ~1e4 cap, so it does not run — while Kahn's is a queue loop with O(1) stack depth and runs fine. So a choice that is stylistic in C++ is a FEASIBILITY choice in JavaScript: the iterative algorithm is not merely the safer option, it is the option. A downstream agent inheriting the C++ default's indifference between the two would present a broken one as an equal alternative. (A recursive DFS with an explicit stack is the third option and is also fine; the point is that the naive recursion is not.)

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

Per route AR-1 the dependency is recorded here and `status` is "provisional". It is NOT invented as a local anchor, NOT faked as a `prerequisites.roots` edge, and NOT declared as a cross_cluster_attachment — all three are AR-1-forbidden. Why it is above NEU-887's elementary floor: unlike a bare DFS (see cl-2.root-an-unrooted-tree, where SUB-4 judged the traversal to be AT the floor and did NOT open a request), topological order is a named algorithm with a correctness argument that the DP's validity rests on directly. LEDGER: the D-S3 request belongs in ../C005-dp-map-schema/adjudication/01_schema-decision-ledger.md, which is SUB-2's file. SUB-4 is sole writer of this file ONLY and cannot write the ledger without breaking the ownership rule that makes the parallel fan-out safe. The request is therefore carried here and surfaced in NEU-935's PR for SUB-2/OUT-7. Named gap, named owner — not a defect to be discovered.

### `cl-2.interval-evaluation-order-by-length`

**Interval DP is evaluated by increasing length** · aliases: *length-first iteration*, *increasing-span order*

> That the containment order which makes the recurrence acyclic is realized concretely by iterating ranges in increasing length, and that the natural row-major order over the endpoint table does NOT respect it — a range's dependencies are shorter ranges, which are scattered across the table rather than adjacent to it. This is the first place in the map where the evaluation order is not the obvious loop order, and knowing why is what generalizes to the tree and DAG families.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.interval-indexed-state-space` — Interval-indexed state space

`cl-2.implement-interval-dp-length-loop`, `cl-2.palindromic-interval-dp`

### `cl-2.interval-indexed-state-space`

**Interval-indexed state space** · aliases: *range DP state*, *substring DP state*

> The state space whose subproblems are contiguous ranges of a sequence, identified by their two endpoints, and the structural claim that makes it DP: a range's answer depends only on the answers of ranges strictly contained in it, so containment gives a partial order with no cycles. What a learner acquires is the shift from "index into the sequence" to "a subproblem IS a subrange" — the first state in the map that is not a position.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-2.interval-evaluation-order-by-length`, `cl-2.interval-split-recurrence`, `cl-2.palindromic-interval-dp`, `cl-2.recognize-interval-dp-under-an-unfamiliar-surface`

### `cl-2.interval-split-recurrence`

**Interval split recurrence** · aliases: *range merge recurrence*, *O(n^3) interval DP*

> The recurrence that minimizes or maximizes over every way of cutting a range into two adjacent subranges, plus a cost of joining them, and the cubic complexity that follows from three nested ranges of parameters. ### `cl-2.interval-state-with-boundary-context`

**Interval state augmented with boundary context** · aliases: *3D interval DP*, *interval DP with an extra dimension*, *remove-boxes pattern*

> That a range's endpoints are sometimes NOT a sufficient state, because what happens inside a range depends on what survives outside it or on what has already been removed around it — burst balloons, removing boxes, merging with a carried count. The repair is a third state axis carrying the boundary context, and the fact a learner acquires is diagnostic: when the two halves of a split are NOT independent given the split, the state is under-specified and the recurrence is wrong rather than merely slow.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.interval-split-recurrence` — Interval split recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.split-point-semantics` — What the split point means: the last-operation view

`cl-2.augment-an-insufficient-interval-state`

### `cl-2.item-selection-state-space`

**Item-selection state space** · aliases: *selection DP state*, *prefix-and-capacity state*

> The state space of a selection DP: a subproblem is identified by which items are still available to decide (canonically a prefix of the item list) together with how much of a bounded resource remains. The key fact is WHY a prefix suffices — the items already decided influence the future only through the resource consumed, so the exponential set of chosen subsets collapses to a two-axis table. Acquiring this is acquiring why 2^n subsets need not be enumerated.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.counting-vs-optimizing-objective`, `cl-2.pseudo-polynomial-complexity`, `cl-2.recognize-knapsack-under-an-unfamiliar-surface`, `cl-2.tree-knapsack-merge`, `cl-2.unbounded-knapsack-recurrence`, `cl-2.zero-one-knapsack-recurrence`

### `cl-2.longest-path-in-a-dag`

**Longest path in a DAG** · aliases: *critical path*, *longest path DAG*

> The canonical explicit-DAG DP, and the contrast that gives it its weight: longest path is NP-hard on a general graph and linear on a DAG, and the only thing that changed is acyclicity. That contrast is what a learner acquires — acyclicity is not a technicality of the recurrence, it is the entire reason the problem is tractable, which retroactively explains why every DP formulation must establish it.

### `cl-2.matrix-chain-recurrence`

**Matrix-chain multiplication recurrence** · aliases: *matrix chain order*, *optimal parenthesization*

> The canonical interval DP: parenthesize a chain of matrix products to minimize scalar multiplications, where a range's cost is the best split's two subcosts plus the cost of the final product, itself determined by the dimensions at the range's boundary. Its instructive property is that the joining cost depends only on the range's endpoints and the split — the simplest possible join, which is why the family is usually taught here first.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.interval-split-recurrence` — Interval split recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.split-point-semantics` — What the split point means: the last-operation view

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-2.optimal-bst-recurrence`

**Optimal binary search tree recurrence** · aliases: *optimal BST*, *Knuth's optimal binary search tree*

> The weighted interval DP in which the split point is the subtree ROOT and the joining cost is the total access frequency of the range — so choosing a root pushes every key in the range one level deeper, and the cost is a sum over the range rather than a function of its endpoints. It is the family's second shape and the reason the family is more than matrix chain: the join now depends on the range's CONTENTS.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.interval-split-recurrence` — Interval split recurrence
- `intra`   → `cl-2.split-point-semantics` — What the split point means: the last-operation view

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-2.palindromic-interval-dp`

**Palindromic range DP** · aliases: *longest palindromic subsequence*, *palindrome partitioning DP*

> The range DP over a string in which a range's value depends on whether its two boundary characters match — longest palindromic subsequence, minimum insertions to make a palindrome, palindrome partitioning. Its shape is a genuine variant, not an instance of the split recurrence: the transition peels the two ENDS rather than enumerating an interior split, so it is quadratic rather than cubic and the "last operation" is at the boundary.

- `intra`   → `cl-2.interval-indexed-state-space` — Interval-indexed state space

- `cross`   → `cl-1.lcs-dp` — Longest common subsequence DP  *(CL-2→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-2.palindromic-interval-dp->cl-1.lcs-dp`)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-2.per-node-state-dimension`

**Per-vertex state dimension** · aliases: *dp[v][0/1]*, *tree DP with vertex states*, *independent set on a tree*

> That the subtree index is often not a sufficient state and must be crossed with a small per-vertex condition — "the best in v's subtree GIVEN v is taken / not taken", given v's colour, given v is matched. The reason is structural rather than incidental: a constraint that couples a vertex to its parent (adjacent vertices may not both be chosen) is invisible inside the subtree, so the subtree must report an answer for each of the parent's possible decisions rather than one answer. Maximum independent set on a tree is the canonical instance.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.subtree-indexed-state-space` — Subtree-indexed state space
- `intra`   → `cl-2.child-aggregation` — Child aggregation
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-2.prove-interval-dp-correctness`

**Prove an interval DP correct by induction on span**

> Argue that enumerating splits yields the optimum: establish that every solution HAS a last operation (an exhaustiveness claim about the solution space, and the step that is usually skipped), that it induces the split, and that the halves are independent given it. Independence is where real interval problems fail this argument — and recognizing the failure is what forces an augmented state, which is the boundary-context node below.

**Type** — `skill` / `proof`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.split-point-semantics` — What the split point means: the last-operation view

`cl-2.augment-an-insufficient-interval-state`

### `cl-2.prove-tree-knapsack-quadratic-bound`

**Prove the subtree-size-bounded merge runs in quadratic time**

> Establish that the tree knapsack's merge — whose loops are bounded by the sizes of the two subtrees being merged rather than by the global capacity — costs quadratic time in total rather than the cubic a naive reading suggests. The argument is the acquisition: each pair of vertices is charged exactly once, at their lowest common ancestor. Without it a learner cannot tell a correct tree knapsack from one that is too slow, because the two differ only in a loop bound.

**Type** — `skill` / `proof`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.tree-knapsack-merge` — Tree knapsack: merging children's size-indexed vectors
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

Empty corpus_refs is systematic for `proof` nodes.

### `cl-2.pseudo-polynomial-complexity`

**Pseudo-polynomiality** · aliases: *pseudo-polynomial time*, *weakly polynomial DP*

> That O(n·W) is polynomial in the VALUE of the capacity, not in its encoding length, and what follows: the knapsack DP is efficient exactly when W is small, and the same DP is useless when W is a 64-bit number even though n is tiny. The fact a learner needs in order to know when the table is the wrong tool — and the reason 0/1 knapsack is NP-hard and yet routinely solved.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.item-selection-state-space` — Item-selection state space

**Required by**

`cl-2.formulate-zero-one-knapsack`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `1` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `2` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-2` |
| `proof_obligation_load` | `1` |
| `recognition_load` | `3` |
| `state_formulation_load` | `1` |
| `transition_derivation_load` | `1` |

*`creator_review: "deferred-provisional"` — the creator plausibility review did NOT run (charter
Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — pseudo-polynomiality is precisely the property that makes the CONSTANT FACTOR decide feasibility, so this is the one complexity-knowledge node where the language's performance envelope is part of the content rather than a footnote. The whole point of the node is that the O(nW) bound is not polynomial in the input SIZE, so whether a solution passes turns on how large an nW product the runtime clears — a quantity that is language-dependent by definition. The nW a JavaScript solution clears is smaller than the C++ default's, and JS-E5 (a flat typed array for the capacity table rather than a boxed Array) is what recovers most of the gap. A downstream agent who inherits a C++ author's intuition for 'W up to about this much is fine' will state a threshold that does not hold. Recorded per JS-D1's exception: this is not blanket JS-E6 dilution, it is the node whose subject IS the constant-factor boundary. DIRECTIONAL ONLY — the audit does not and may not quantify the ratio (JS-U2, benchmarking out of scope).

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-2.recognize-an-implicit-dag`

**Recognize an implicit DAG in a problem that mentions no graph** · aliases: *implicit graph DP*, *states-as-vertices reading*, *DP as longest path*

> Build the DAG that a problem does not mention: identify what a "situation" is, what a legal move between situations is, and check that no sequence of moves returns to a situation. The canonical instance is longest increasing subsequence, which is a longest path in the DAG on indices with an edge wherever a later index carries a larger value — the same problem read two ways. What a learner acquires is that this construction is always available, and that its acyclicity check is where a wrong state declares itself.

**Type** — `skill` / `transfer`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.dp-state-graph-is-a-dag` — Every DP's state graph is a DAG
- `intra`   → `cl-2.longest-path-in-a-dag` — Longest path in a DAG
- `intra`   → `cl-2.cyclic-state-dependency-breaks-dp` — A cyclic state dependency breaks the recurrence
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-optimal-substructure` — Recognize optimal substructure in a problem  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-overlapping-subproblems` — Recognize overlapping subproblems and distinguish DP from divide-and-conquer  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.longest-increasing-subsequence-quadratic` — Longest increasing subsequence, O(n^2) recurrence  *(CL-2→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-2.recognize-an-implicit-dag->cl-1.longest-increasing-subsequence-quadratic`)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-2.recognize-rerooting-applicability`

**Recognize when rerooting applies and when the aggregate forbids it**

> Decide whether a tree DP can be rerooted at all. Two conditions, both easily assumed rather than checked: the problem must actually want a per-root answer, and the child aggregate must be re-computable with one child withheld. A learner acquires the check for the second — is the fold invertible; if not, are prefix-suffix aggregates affordable — before writing two passes that silently produce wrong up-values.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.rerooting-principle` — Rerooting principle
- `intra`   → `cl-2.exclude-one-child-aggregate` — Excluding one child from an aggregate
- `intra`   → `cl-2.formulate-subtree-recurrence` — Formulate a subtree recurrence: decide what a subtree must summarize
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.implement-rerooting-two-pass-dfs`

### `cl-2.reconstruct-interval-witness`

**Reconstruct the optimal parenthesization from an interval table**

> Walk the table to emit the actual grouping — the parenthesization, the merge order, the BST — rather than only its cost, by recursing on the stored split of each range. The distinction from the knapsack witness is real and is why this is its own node: the output is a TREE recovered from a table indexed by ranges, not a subset, so the walk is recursive on both halves rather than a single back-chain.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.implement-interval-dp-length-loop` — Implement an interval DP over a length-major loop
- `intra`   → `cl-2.split-point-semantics` — What the split point means: the last-operation view
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |
| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `3` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `6` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-4` |
| `proof_obligation_load` | `0` |

| `state_formulation_load` | `2` |
| `transition_derivation_load` | `2` |

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **JavaScript-neutral** (assessed, explicitly not material)
- JavaScript-neutral, and deliberately so despite being a recursive walk. The parenthesization is recovered by recursing on the two halves of each split, so the depth is bounded by the interval nesting — and interval DP's own O(n^3) budget caps n at the low thousands, putting the depth around 1e3 at worst against a cap on the order of 1e4 (JS-E1). The recursion is real but the cubic time bound forecloses the sizes that would make it dangerous. This is a non-claim on JS-E1 stated explicitly rather than left silent: the technique's own complexity, not the language, is what keeps it safe.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-2.reconstruct-knapsack-witness`

**Reconstruct the chosen item set from a knapsack table** · aliases: *path reconstruction*, *witness recovery*, *traceback*

> Recover a witness, not just an optimum: walk the table backwards from the answering state, at each step deciding which incoming transition was taken, and emit the item set. The idea a learner acquires is that the table already contains the witness and the collapse to one array is what destroys it — which is why the space-saving and the reconstruction requirements conflict, and why that conflict must be resolved at formulation time rather than discovered afterwards.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.zero-one-knapsack-recurrence` — 0/1 knapsack recurrence
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.memoization-vs-tabulation` — Memoization vs tabulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.debug-item-reuse-from-loop-direction`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-2.rerooting-principle`

**Rerooting principle** · aliases: *rerooting*, *all-roots DP*, *up-down DP*, *second-pass tree DP*

> That the answer for EVERY vertex as root is obtainable in one pass more than the answer for one, because a vertex's view of the tree splits into its own subtree (already computed downward) and everything else (derivable from its parent's corresponding value). The decomposition — down-value plus up-value — is the whole idea, and it converts a quadratic recompute-per-root into two traversals.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.subtree-indexed-state-space` — Subtree-indexed state space
- `intra`   → `cl-2.formulate-subtree-recurrence` — Formulate a subtree recurrence: decide what a subtree must summarize
- `intra`   → `cl-2.child-aggregation` — Child aggregation
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-2.exclude-one-child-aggregate`, `cl-2.prove-rerooting-correctness`, `cl-2.recognize-rerooting-applicability`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-2.root-an-unrooted-tree`

**Root an unrooted tree** · aliases: *parent assignment*, *orienting a tree*

> Turn an undirected acyclic input into the rooted structure the DP needs: pick an arbitrary vertex, traverse, and record each vertex's parent so the adjacency list can be read as a child list. Small, mechanical, and its own node because it is a real precondition a learner executes before every tree DP and can execute wrongly — most commonly by recursing back into the parent and turning a tree into an infinite walk.

**Type** — `skill` / `procedural`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.subtree-indexed-state-space` — Subtree-indexed state space

**Required by**

`cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs`

| Dimension | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |
| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `2` |

*`creator_review: "deferred-provisional"` — the creator plausibility review did NOT run (charter
Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — rooting is a traversal, and the recursive traversal that is the C++ default does not survive the JavaScript stack at competitive tree sizes. The C++ idiom is a recursive DFS assigning parents and depths; on a path-shaped tree with n = 2e5 it descends 2e5 frames, which a typical 8 MB process stack absorbs and which some judges raise further — platform-dependent, not guaranteed. JavaScript's cap is ~1e4 frames and there is NO equivalent knob a submitted solution can set — V8's --stack-size is a host flag, not something the program controls. A path-shaped tree is the standard adversarial input, not an exotic one, so the failure is expected rather than unlucky. The JavaScript realization must therefore root the tree with an EXPLICIT stack (or a BFS order) — not as a stylistic preference but as the only form that runs. This node is upstream of every tree-DP node in the cluster, so the effect propagates.

### `cl-2.select-knapsack-variant`

**Select the knapsack variant a statement actually describes**

> Read the multiplicity and the objective out of a statement and pick: 0/1, bounded, unbounded, or pure feasibility — and separately, count-ways vs optimize. *nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**What the split point means: the last-operation view** · aliases: *last-operation decomposition*, *final-move view*

> That the enumerated split is not "where to cut" but "which operation happens LAST" — the final multiplication in a matrix chain, the root of a subtree in an optimal BST, the last merge of two piles. ### `cl-2.subset-sum-feasibility`

**Subset-sum feasibility** · aliases: *subset sum*, *partition problem*, *reachable-sums DP*

> The boolean specialization: rather than maximize a value, decide which totals are reachable by some subset. The state collapses to a reachability set and the transition to a shift-and-union. ### `cl-2.subtree-indexed-state-space`

**Subtree-indexed state space** · aliases: *tree DP state*, *DP over subtrees*

> The state space whose subproblem is "the subtree rooted at v", and the two facts that make it the friendliest structured family: rooting the tree induces a containment order with no cycles for free, and a subtree's answer depends only on its children's — so the whole DP is one traversal and the evaluation-order problem that dominates interval DP disappears. What a learner acquires is that the tree IS the recursion, rather than a structure the recursion must be fitted to.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

### `cl-2.tree-diameter-via-dp`

**Tree diameter as a subtree DP** · aliases: *diameter of a tree*, *path-through-vertex decomposition*, *best-two-children pattern*

> The pattern in which the answer is not at the root but at SOME vertex: a longest path has a highest point, and at that point it is the sum of the two deepest child descents. So the DP returns the deepest descent while accumulating a global best over each vertex's best two children. The transferable idea is decomposing a global object by its topmost vertex — the tree family's counterpart of the interval family's last-operation view, and the reason both belong in one cluster.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.subtree-indexed-state-space` — Subtree-indexed state space
- `intra`   → `cl-2.child-aggregation` — Child aggregation
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-2.unbounded-knapsack-recurrence`

**Unbounded knapsack recurrence** · aliases: *complete knapsack*, *integer knapsack with repetition*

> The recurrence in which an item may be taken any number of times, and the structural change that licenses it: the transition for capacity w reads the state at w minus the item's weight for the SAME item set rather than the prefix without it. The item axis stops being a prefix of decisions and becomes a choice of "which item to use next", which is what LICENSES the collapse to one dimension — an available optimization, not a forced one.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.item-selection-state-space` — Item-selection state space

### `cl-2.zero-one-knapsack-recurrence`

**0/1 knapsack recurrence** · aliases: *binary knapsack*, *0-1 knapsack*

> The take-or-leave recurrence over an item prefix and a remaining capacity, and the specific fact that makes it 0/1: the recurrence for item i reads only states computed for the prefix WITHOUT item i, so no optimal solution can contain the same item twice. The at-most-once constraint is enforced by the shape of the transition, not by a check.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-2.item-selection-state-space` — Item-selection state space
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-2.bounded-knapsack-multiplicity`, `cl-2.descending-vs-ascending-capacity-loop`, `cl-2.formulate-zero-one-knapsack`, `cl-2.prove-zero-one-knapsack-correctness`, `cl-2.reconstruct-knapsack-witness`, `cl-2.select-knapsack-variant`, `cl-2.subset-sum-feasibility`, `cl-2.tree-knapsack-merge`

## CL-3 — State-compression / specialized-domain

*DP whose defining contribution is a non-tuple state encoding, or which is bound to a specialized problem domain with its own semantics. The INDETERMINATE SINK of Convention U2 (D-F4 §4).*

**31 nodes** · files: `nodes/cl-3-state-compression.yaml`

### `cl-3.automaton-state-dp-semantics`

**Automaton-state DP semantics** · aliases: *automaton DP*, *DP on a recognizer*, *DP over KMP/Aho-Corasick states*

> A DP's state can be a NODE OF AN AUTOMATON rather than an index into the input. What the encoding asserts: the automaton's state is a complete summary of everything the prefix consumed so far that can still affect the future, so the DP indexes over "where the recognizer currently is" and the automaton's transition function IS the DP's transition. This is the encoding insight — counting strings that avoid a pattern becomes a walk over the pattern's recognizer, and the pattern's structure disappears into the automaton.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.formulate-automaton-dp`, `cl-3.implement-aho-corasick-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding`

**Bitmask state encoding** · aliases: *subset DP state*, *mask DP*, *state compression*

> A subset of a small ground set is representable as the bits of a single integer, and that integer is usable as a DP state index. What the encoding asserts: set membership is bit position, set union is bitwise OR, the state space is exactly 2^n, and the whole subset lattice is addressable by ordinary integer arithmetic. The exponential state count is not a defect of the encoding — it is the honest size of the subset lattice, and it is why the technique is bounded to roughly n <= 20-22 in practice.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-2.subset-sum-feasibility` — Subset-sum feasibility  *(CL-3→CL-2, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding->cl-2.subset-sum-feasibility`)*

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E4`
- MATERIAL — this node IS the bit encoding, and JavaScript's bit width is half C++'s with a silent failure at the boundary. C++ encodes a subset in an int (32 bits) or unsigned long long (64), reflexively and without a ceiling worth thinking about. JavaScript's bitwise operators (&, |, ^, ~, <<, >>) coerce BOTH OPERANDS TO INT32 and return int32, so an encoded mask has 31 safely usable bits — 32 only with >>> 0 discipline maintained everywhere, which breaks composition with << and & afterwards. Two specific hazards a downstream agent must write, neither of which announces itself: `1 << 31` is NEGATIVE (-2147483648), so a mask with the top bit set cannot index an array and compares wrongly; and SHIFT COUNTS ARE TAKEN MOD 32, so `1 << 32` silently evaluates to 1 rather than 2^32 — it does not throw, does not saturate, and yields a completely different mask. Recorded as correctness-risk rather than blocking, deliberately: the common bitmask DP has n <= 20 because 2^n MEMORY binds long before the bit width does, so the 31-bit ceiling is genuinely NOT reached in the standard case and claiming otherwise would be invented. The risk is real at the boundary and the ceiling is half of C++'s; both facts belong on this node.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only, not a verdict (OUT-5/SUB-8 owns that): JavaScript's bitwise operators coerce to signed 32-bit, so a mask over n > 30 elements silently misbehaves in a way it does not in the C++ competitive default the selected references assume. BigInt or a two-word split is the workaround and both change the cost model. SUB-8 must reach its own verdict.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-3.broken-profile-state-encoding`

**Broken-profile state encoding** · aliases: *broken profile DP*, *profile DP*, *frontier DP*, *contour DP*

> A DP over a grid can advance CELL BY CELL rather than row by row, carrying a "broken" profile — a mask describing the frontier between decided and undecided cells, which mid-row is a boundary that steps down one column. What the encoding asserts: the frontier is a complete separator, so everything behind it is forgettable and everything ahead depends on the grid only through it. This is what cuts a row-by-row profile DP's transition cost from enumerating all compatible row pairs to a constant per cell.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding` — Bitmask state encoding
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.implement-plug-dp`, `cl-3.plug-dp-connectivity-encoding`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E4`
- MATERIAL — broken-profile encodings are bit-packed and sit closer to JavaScript's 31-bit ceiling than an ordinary subset mask does. The profile carries one bit per column plus the broken-cell marker, so the mask width is the grid's WIDTH + 1 rather than a small item count. Widths up to ~20 fit inside JavaScript's 31 usable bits, so the common case is safe and this is not claimed as blocking. But the C++ default has 64 bits with no ceiling worth reasoning about, whereas the JavaScript author is working at width+1 bits against a hard 31 with a SILENT failure past it: shift counts are taken mod 32, so `1 << 32` yields 1 rather than 2^32, producing a wrong mask with no error. The margin that C++ never has to think about is the content of this verdict.

- **JavaScript-neutral** (assessed, explicitly not material)
- JavaScript-neutral, and the XOR question is checked rather than assumed — this is a deliberate non-claim. Sprague-Grundy composition is an XOR fold, and JavaScript's ^ is a 32-bit SIGNED operation (JS-E4), which is the kind of thing that looks like a finding. It is not one here: Grundy values are bounded by the branching of the game graph and sit far below 2^31 in every game this technique is posed with, so the XOR is exact and the sign bit is never reached. Asserting a hazard would be inventing one. The mex computation is a small-set scan over the same small values. Recorded as neutral WITH the check stated, so a reader can see the boundary was examined rather than skipped.

**Diagnose a bitmask DP that TLEs or MLEs**

> Work back from the characteristic failure signatures of a bitmask DP to their causes: distinguish a state space genuinely too large from one carrying a dimension the transition never reads; recognize 3^n submask enumeration where the intended complexity was 2^n * n; spot the redundant popcount in the inner loop. **JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — the node diagnoses a bitmask DP that TLEs or MLEs, and in JavaScript the MLE branch of that diagnosis has a cause C++ does not have. In C++ an MLE means the state space is genuinely too big: vector<int> is 4 bytes a cell and there is no overhead to blame, so the only remedy is to shrink the state. In JavaScript an MLE at the SAME state count may mean nothing more than that the table is a boxed Array rather than an Int32Array (JS-E5) — a container mistake, not a modelling mistake, with a fix that costs one line and does not touch the formulation. So the JavaScript differential diagnosis gains a branch that must be checked FIRST, before concluding the state space needs redesigning: a learner who inherits the C++ taxonomy will start redesigning a state that was fine. The TLE branch shifts the same way for the boxed-array constant factor. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).

### `cl-3.digit-dp-state-semantics`

**Digit DP state semantics** · aliases: *digit DP*, *numeral-prefix DP*, *counting-with-bound DP*

> What a digit-DP state means: a prefix of the numeral is fixed, and the rest of the number is free subject to flags that summarize the prefix's relationship to the bound. The three canonical flags and what each asserts — `tight` (the prefix equals the bound's prefix, so the next digit is capped), `started` (a nonzero digit has appeared, distinguishing a leading zero from a real zero), and the problem-specific carry of whatever property is being counted. The insight the encoding rests on: the set of numbers <= N is not an interval the DP can iterate, but it IS a prefix tree the DP can walk, and the flags are exactly the finite summary that makes the walk's states collide.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.overlapping-subproblems` — Overlapping subproblems  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.digit-dp-bound-correctness`, `cl-3.formulate-digit-dp`, `cl-3.implement-digit-dp`, `cl-3.recognize-digit-dp-under-unfamiliar-surface`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-3.expectation-dp-well-definedness`

**Argue an expectation DP is well-defined**

> Establish that the answer the DP computes is the answer that exists: that the process reaches an absorbing state with probability 1, that the expectation is therefore finite, and that the linear system the states induce is nonsingular so its solution is unique. What a learner acquires is the obligation itself — knowing that a finite number coming out of the table is not evidence the expectation was finite.

**Type** — `skill` / `proof`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.formulate-expectation-dp` — Formulate an expectation DP
- `intra`   → `cl-3.linearity-of-expectation-in-dp` — Linearity of expectation as a DP-licensing property
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.linear-algebra` — Linear algebra (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-3.formulate-automaton-dp`

**Formulate a DP over an automaton's states**

> Turn a pattern-constrained counting or optimization problem into a DP over a recognizer: choose the automaton that summarizes the constraint, take its node set as the state space (usually crossed with a length and the problem's own dimensions), mark the accepting/forbidden nodes as absorbing or excluded, and fix the base case at the automaton's root. The choice that matters is WHICH recognizer — and that choice is forced by the constraint's shape.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.automaton-state-dp-semantics` — Automaton-state DP semantics
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-overlapping-subproblems` — Recognize overlapping subproblems and distinguish DP from divide-and-conquer  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)  *(CL-3→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-3.formulate-automaton-dp->cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d`)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.implement-aho-corasick-dp`, `cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp`

Run D-F4 §3's cascade on it and no test fires, because it is not a DP technique at all — it is general-algorithms machinery above NEU-887's elementary floor, which is precisely the register's admission rule (a)+(b)+(c). - Interim status: "provisional" per the register's interim_node_status. NOT DONE, per the register's `forbidden` list — recorded explicitly so the audit can confirm the boundary held rather than trust it: - NOT invented locally (a local anchor is invisible to every other mapper and to the floor audit); - NOT faked as a prerequisites.roots edge (that would launder a non-DP dependency into the DP floor — "exactly what the floor audit hunts"); - NOT declared as a cross_cluster_attachment (an anchor is not a sibling cluster's node; SUB-12 would correctly report it unresolvable); - NOT silently dropped (a smoothed gap; forbidden by NEU-887's status discipline, inherited). The DP-side prerequisites ARE drawn normally — this node's chain reaches cl-1.root.* — so the node is not floating. What is missing is the non-DP limb, and it is missing VISIBLY.

### `cl-3.formulate-digit-dp`

**Formulate a digit DP**

> Turn a "count the numbers in a range satisfying P" statement into a concrete digit-DP formulation: decide the digit order, decide which flags the state carries, decide the finite summary of P that the state must remember, fix the base case at the numeral's end, and reduce a two-sided range to two one-sided counts by f(R) - f(L-1). The choice that matters is what to forget.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.digit-dp-state-semantics` — Digit DP state semantics
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-overlapping-subproblems` — Recognize overlapping subproblems and distinguish DP from divide-and-conquer  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.counting-dp-over-linear-domain` — Counting DP over a linear or rectangular domain, modulo a prime  *(CL-3→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-3.formulate-digit-dp->cl-1.counting-dp-over-linear-domain`)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.digit-dp-bound-correctness`, `cl-3.implement-digit-dp`, `cl-3.recognize-digit-dp-under-unfamiliar-surface`

### `cl-3.formulate-expectation-dp`

**Formulate an expectation DP**

> Turn a random process into a concrete correct expectation recurrence: identify the state the process is memoryless over, choose the direction the value accumulates in, write E[state] as the probability-weighted sum over successors plus the step cost, and fix the terminal base case at zero. The choice that matters is the direction — and it is forced by what the value means, not free.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.linearity-of-expectation-in-dp` — Linearity of expectation as a DP-licensing property
- `intra`   → `cl-3.probability-vs-expectation-dp-semantics` — Probability DP vs expectation DP
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-optimal-substructure` — Recognize optimal substructure in a problem  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.expectation-dp-well-definedness`, `cl-3.resolve-expectation-dp-self-loops`

### `cl-3.formulate-game-dp`

**Formulate a game DP: choose what a position is**

> Turn a two-player game statement into a concrete DP: decide what a position is, derive the move relation, check impartiality and normal play, decide whether the value is boolean, Grundy, or a score under minimax, and identify the terminal base cases. The decisive choice is whether the game is a SUM of independent games — because if it is, Sprague-Grundy collapses the state space, and if it is not, the k-dimensional state is unavoidable.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.win-lose-position-semantics` — Winning and losing position semantics
- `intra`   → `cl-3.sprague-grundy-theorem` — The Sprague-Grundy theorem
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.recognize-optimal-substructure` — Recognize optimal substructure in a problem  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.prove-game-decomposition-via-grundy`, `cl-3.recognize-impartial-game-under-unfamiliar-surface`

### `cl-3.grundy-number-and-mex`

**Grundy number and the mex operator** · aliases: *Grundy value*, *nimber*, *minimum excludant*

> The Grundy value of a position is the minimum excludant (mex) of its successors' Grundy values — the smallest non-negative integer not among them. What this buys over a boolean: Grundy 0 is exactly a losing position, so the Grundy value strictly refines win/lose, and the refinement is what makes games COMPOSABLE. The mex is not an arbitrary choice of aggregator; it is forced by the requirement that a position's value behave like a Nim-heap size.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.win-lose-position-semantics` — Winning and losing position semantics

**Required by**

`cl-3.compute-grundy-values`, `cl-3.sprague-grundy-theorem`

- MATERIAL — the goto table is the artifact, and JavaScript forces a container decision C++ does not. The automaton's transition table is |states| x |alphabet| and it is read once per (position, state) in the DP's innermost loop — a hot, dense, integer-valued table, which is exactly where JS-E5 has its largest lever: a flat Int32Array with state * ALPHA + c addressing, versus the array-of-arrays the C++ vector<array<int, 26>> transcribes to. C++ gets the contiguous unboxed layout for free. Deliberate non-claim on JS-E1, worth stating because it is the expectation: Aho-Corasick's failure links are built by a BFS, which is a QUEUE loop with O(1) stack depth — so the automaton construction, unlike most of this map's tree-shaped work, does NOT meet the recursion cap. This node is provisional pending an AR-1 anchor request; the verdict is on the DP-side realization this cluster owns, and the anchor's own internals are out of scope per DR-S03 (JS-U4). Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation, not a verdict: the goto table is conventionally a flat node-count x alphabet array; in JS a typed array is the realistic carrier and an array-of-objects is not, which is a materially different implementation shape from the references' C++ default. SUB-8 owns the verdict.

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E4`, `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — this node writes both the masks and the table, so it carries both JavaScript effects that bitmask DP meets. JS-E5 is the sharper one and it decides MLE rather than TLE: a bitmask DP's table is 2^n cells (often 2^n * n), and at n = 20 that is 2^20 entries — an Int32Array(1 << 20) is exactly 4 MB, while `new Array(1 << 20)` of boxed numbers costs a multiple of that with no guarantee of the packed-SMI representation, which is lost on a single non-SMI write. C++'s vector<int> IS the 4 MB buffer with no decision to make. So the memory ceiling that decides whether a bitmask DP fits is language-dependent, and the typed array is what recovers the C++ default's behaviour. JS-E4 rides along on the masks this node writes, which are subject to the 31-bit ceiling and the mod-32 shift semantics recorded on cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding — and NEU-936's observation on this node adds a specific instance the audit confirms: THERE IS NO NATIVE POPCOUNT. C++ has __builtin_popcount (a single instruction on any modern target), and bitmask DP uses it constantly — to index by subset size, to bound iteration, to test cardinality. JavaScript has Math.clz32 but nothing for population count, so it must be hand-rolled (an SWAR bit-twiddle, or a precomputed 2^16 lookup table) — and the SWAR form is itself written in 32-bit bitwise operators, inheriting JS-E4's ceiling. That is index arithmetic the C++ author never writes. JS-E6 is recorded only in combination with the typed-array idiom, per JS-D1. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation, not a verdict: a 2^20-entry typed array is routine in JS, but the signed-32-bit bitwise coercion caps n at 30 and `Math.clz32`/popcount have no native primitive, so the index arithmetic differs materially from the C++ default the references assume. SUB-8 owns the verdict.

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E3`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING, at the INPUT BOUNDARY rather than inside the recurrence — a place a C++-derived version of this node has no reason to mention. Digit DP's whole purpose is counting numbers up to a bound, and the bounds it is posed with are routinely up to 1e18, because that is what fits in C++'s long long — the technique's canonical problem sizes are SHAPED BY the C++ integer width. 1e18 is ~111x past 2^53. So in JavaScript the bound CANNOT BE READ INTO A Number at all: parseInt/Number on '1000000000000000000' silently returns the nearest representable double, and the digits the DP then enumerates are the digits of the WRONG NUMBER. C++'s cin >> n into a long long is exact and the C++ author never thinks about it. The JavaScript realization must keep the bound as a STRING (which digit DP wants anyway — it iterates the decimal digits, and a string is already a digit array) or as a BigInt. This is blocking and it is invisible: the DP itself is correct, the state semantics are correct, and the answer is wrong because the input was destroyed before the DP started. Deliberate non-claim on JS-E1: digit DP recurses once per digit, so its depth is at most ~19 frames for a 1e18 bound — the recursion cap is nowhere near it, and the memoized top-down form that is the natural realization is entirely safe here.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation, not a verdict: the natural top-down realization recurses to numeral length, which is shallow, so JS's fixed stack is not the constraint here that it is elsewhere. Recorded because a reader may expect the root's recursion-depth observation to propagate, and here it does not. SUB-8 owns the verdict.

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E4`, `JS-E7`, `JS-E3`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — this node writes both the encoding and the state map, and JavaScript breaks both. The encoding: per cl-3.plug-dp-connectivity-encoding, a multi-bit-per-plug mask exceeds 31 bits at the widths plug DP is posed at, so the bitwise operators cannot hold it (JS-E4) and the realization must move to BigInt or a digit/string encoding (JS-E3). The state map compounds it (JS-E7): plug DP keeps a HASH MAP from live packed state to value, rebuilt per cell, and that map's key type is exactly what JavaScript has no good answer for. C++ uses unordered_map<unsigned long long, T> — keyed by value, 64 bits of key, fast. JavaScript has no value-typed key: a Map keyed on a BigInt works but hashes a heap object per lookup; a Map keyed on a string allocates a string per lookup in the innermost loop; and packing into a Number to key on drops straight back into the 2^53 and int32 ceilings the encoding was trying to escape. Every JavaScript option is materially worse than the C++ default, in the hottest loop of the technique. Both effects land on the same node, which is why this is the most JavaScript-affected implementation node in the map.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation, not a verdict: reachable frontier states are sparse and are conventionally held in a hash map keyed by the profile. JS `Map` boxes keys and its iteration order/allocation behaviour differ materially from the `unordered_map` the references assume; a 4-bit-per-cell profile also exceeds the 32-bit bitwise window fast. SUB-8 owns the verdict.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-3.implement-steiner-tree-dp`

**Implement a Steiner-tree DP**

> Realize the Dreyfus-Wagner recurrence: iterate terminal masks in increasing order, perform the submask-pair merge for each root vertex, then relax the whole layer along graph edges to fixpoint before advancing. What a learner acquires is the two-phase realization and the layer-ordering judgment that makes it correct.

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E8`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL — the technique's inner engine is a Dijkstra relaxation over each subset layer, and JavaScript has no priority queue. C++'s priority_queue is stdlib, and the canonical Steiner-tree DP is WRITTEN in terms of it: for each terminal subset, relax the 2^k x n table by running Dijkstra over the vertex dimension. JavaScript ships Array, Map, and Set — Set is insertion-ordered, not sorted — and NO binary heap, no priority queue, no ordered map (JS-E8). So the JavaScript realization must hand-roll a binary heap (over a typed array, to avoid paying JS-E5 in the heap itself) before it can write a line of the actual DP. That is a real prerequisite acquisition the C++ author does not have, and it changes the shape and length of any exercise built on this node. JS-E5 rides along for the 2^k x n table, which wants a flat Int32Array. Recorded as idiom-shift rather than blocking: the technique is entirely feasible in JavaScript, it just is not the same artifact.

### `cl-3.linearity-of-expectation-in-dp`

**Linearity of expectation as a DP-licensing property**

> E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y] holds WITHOUT independence, and this is precisely what licenses an expectation DP to sum over transitions the way an optimizing DP sums over subproblems. This is the family's analogue of optimal substructure: it is the property that makes the decomposition valid at all. Its most consequential corollary is negative — because linearity needs no independence, a learner may correctly decompose dependent events, which is exactly the step that looks illegal and is not.

### `cl-3.plug-dp-connectivity-encoding`

**Plug DP connectivity encoding** · aliases: *plug DP*, *connection profile DP*, *bracket-sequence profile*, *Chinese: cha-tou DP*

> When a grid DP must maintain CONNECTIVITY — closed loops, Hamiltonian paths, spanning structures — the frontier mask is not enough, because two cells being occupied says nothing about whether they belong to the same component. The plug encoding carries, per frontier cell, which component its dangling edge belongs to: as a bracket sequence when the components are planar and cannot interleave, or as a minimal representation under a canonical relabeling in general. What a learner acquires is why a plain mask FAILS here — the specific failure that motivates the whole encoding — and what the bracket sequence's non-crossing property buys.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.broken-profile-state-encoding` — Broken-profile state encoding
- `intra`   → `cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding` — Bitmask state encoding
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.implement-plug-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |

| `implementation_load` | `2` |
