Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E4`, `JS-E3`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the sharpest bit-width finding in the map, and the one place where JavaScript's 32-bit bitwise operators make the C++ default OUTRIGHT INFEASIBLE rather than merely tight. Plug DP does not pack one bit per column; it packs a CONNECTIVITY LABEL per column — 2 to 4 bits each, because a plug must record WHICH component it belongs to, not merely that it exists. So the mask width is (bits per plug) x (grid width + 1), not the width itself. At 3 bits per plug a width-10 grid needs 33 bits; at 4 bits per plug it needs 44; a width-12 grid at 4 bits needs 52. ALL OF THESE EXCEED JAVASCRIPT'S 31 USABLE BITS, and they do so at widths plug DP is routinely posed at. C++ carries them in an unsigned long long (64 bits) without comment — the technique's canonical encoding is SHAPED BY the availability of a 64-bit integer. JavaScript's bitwise operators cannot represent these masks at all: the operands are coerced to int32 before the operation, so the high bits are gone before the shift happens, silently. The JavaScript realization must abandon the bitwise encoding entirely and move to BigInt masks (correct, and materially slower — JS-E3, in an inner loop), a base-k digit array, or a string key. That is not a translation of the C++ idiom, it is a different encoding, and it interacts with JS-E7 because these masks are hash-map keys (cl-3.implement-plug-dp).

### `cl-3.probability-vs-expectation-dp-semantics`

**Probability DP vs expectation DP** · aliases: *probability DP*, *expected-value DP*, *randomized-process DP*

> What the VALUE in the table means, and why the two readings are different techniques wearing one name. A probability DP's value is P(state is reached), accumulates FORWARD from a base case of 1 at the start, and its transitions push mass outward. An expectation DP's value is E[future cost | in this state], accumulates BACKWARD from a base case of 0 at the terminal, and its transitions pull mass inward. The direction reversal is not a stylistic choice — it follows from what the value asserts, and mixing the two is the family's defining confusion.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.formulate-expectation-dp`, `cl-3.linearity-of-expectation-in-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| `entry_gate` | `gate-a` |

| `prerequisite_depth` | `1` |

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **JavaScript-neutral** (assessed, explicitly not material)
- JavaScript-NEUTRAL, and this is a deliberate, load-bearing non-claim rather than an omission. 'JavaScript numbers are imprecise' is folklore, and it is FALSE at this boundary: JavaScript's Number IS IEEE-754 binary64, bit for bit the same type as C++'s double, with the same rounding, the same epsilon, and the same accumulation behaviour. A probability or expectation DP that is numerically fine in C++ double is numerically fine in JavaScript, and one that is ill-conditioned is ill-conditioned in both. Recording a floating-point hazard here would be exactly the invented materiality claim the third acceptance scenario forbids — and it is the invented claim a reviewer would most expect to find. The ONE real difference (C++'s long double has no JavaScript equivalent) is narrow, bites only where near-equal values must be separated, and is recorded there: cl-3.resolve-expectation-dp-self-loops.

### `cl-3.resolve-expectation-dp-self-loops`

**Resolve self-loops and cyclic dependencies in an expectation DP** · aliases: *self-loop elimination*, *Gaussian elimination on DP states*, *cyclic expectation DP*

> Make a cyclic expectation recurrence evaluable. The two realizations a learner acquires: algebraic rearrangement when a state's only cycle is a self-loop (E = p*E + r becomes E = r / (1 - p), and 1 - p != 0 is the condition to check), and assembling the states into a linear system solved by Gaussian elimination when the cycles are genuine and multi-state. Recognizing which case is in front of you, and what each costs, is the substance.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.formulate-expectation-dp` — Formulate an expectation DP
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.linear-algebra` — Linear algebra (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.modular-arithmetic` — Modular arithmetic (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E3`, `JS-E9`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — on TWO independent paths, and NEU-936's mapper observation on this node corrected this audit's first reading of which one dominates. (1) THE MODULAR PATH, which is the common one: competitive expectation problems overwhelmingly demand the answer as a MODULAR RATIONAL — p * q^-1 mod 998244353 — so the self-loop resolution and the cyclic elimination run OVER A FINITE FIELD, not over floats. Dividing by (1 - p_self) is then a modular inverse, and every elimination step is a modmul of two residues below 998244353, whose product reaches ~1e18: ~111x past 2^53 (JS-E2). C++ does it in long long directly. JavaScript silently rounds, so the elimination is wrong from the first step, and JavaScript additionally has NO NATIVE MODULAR INVERSE where C++ solutions inline a fast-power or extended-Euclid over long long — so BigInt or a Montgomery-style routine is forced (JS-E3). This is blocking and it is the dominant case. (2) THE FLOATING-POINT PATH, where the problem does want a real answer: here JavaScript is NOT weaker in the way folklore expects — Number is binary64, bit-identical to C++ double (see cl-3.probability-vs-expectation-dp-semantics). The one real gap is JS-E9a: an elimination that is ill-conditioned as p_self approaches 1 is exactly what competitive C++ answers by switching double to LONG DOUBLE (x87 80-bit) for the extra mantissa bits, and JAVASCRIPT HAS NO WIDER FLOAT. That one-token C++ remedy has no JavaScript counterpart; the author must restructure (pivoting, or reformulating away from the near-singular division). Both paths change what is written, and the modular one blocks.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation, not a verdict: competitive expectation problems usually demand the answer as a modular rational (p * q^-1 mod 998244353), so the elimination runs over a finite field, not over floats. JS has no native modular inverse and its numbers cannot hold the intermediate products, so BigInt or a Montgomery-style routine is forced where C++ uses `long long` directly. SUB-8 owns the verdict.

### `cl-3.sprague-grundy-theorem`

**The Sprague-Grundy theorem** · aliases: *Sprague-Grundy*, *Nim equivalence*, *game sum theorem*

> Every impartial game under normal play is equivalent to a single Nim heap whose size is the position's Grundy value, and the Grundy value of a SUM of independent games is the XOR of their Grundy values. This is the theorem that converts an intractable product state space into a tractable one: k independent subgames need not be a k-dimensional state, because XOR composes them. What a learner acquires is the equivalence and its two preconditions — impartial (both players have the same moves) and normal play (last to move wins) — each of which is routinely violated by problems that look like they qualify.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.grundy-number-and-mex` — Grundy number and the mex operator
- `intra`   → `cl-3.win-lose-position-semantics` — Winning and losing position semantics

**Required by**

`cl-3.compute-grundy-values`, `cl-3.formulate-game-dp`, `cl-3.prove-game-decomposition-via-grundy`, `cl-3.recognize-impartial-game-under-unfamiliar-surface`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

### `cl-3.steiner-tree-dp-state-encoding`

**Steiner-tree DP state encoding** · aliases: *Dreyfus-Wagner*, *Steiner tree DP*, *minimum Steiner tree*

> The minimum Steiner tree over a small terminal set is computable by a DP whose state is (subset of terminals connected, current root vertex) — a mask crossed with a vertex. What the encoding asserts: a partial Steiner tree is fully summarized by WHICH terminals it spans and WHERE it is currently anchored, and the two ways to extend it are the two transitions — merge two disjoint terminal subsets at a shared root, or grow the current tree along a graph edge. The mask over terminals, not the tree structure, is what makes the state space finite.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding` — Bitmask state encoding
- `intra`   → `cl-3.subset-cover-transition-correctness` — Argue a subset-partition transition covers each subset exactly once
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-3.implement-steiner-tree-dp`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **JavaScript-neutral** (assessed, explicitly not material)
- JavaScript-neutral, with the bit-width boundary checked rather than assumed. The state is (subset of terminals, vertex), so the mask is a plain one-bit-per-terminal subset — and the terminal count k is bounded around 10-12 by the 2^k * n state space itself, far below JavaScript's 31 usable bits (JS-E4). Unlike plug DP, this encoding packs ONE bit per position, so the ceiling is not approached and claiming a hazard would be invented. The table's realization is where JavaScript binds, and that verdict is on cl-3.implement-steiner-tree-dp (JS-D2).

### `cl-3.submask-enumeration`

**Submask enumeration** · aliases: *submask iteration*, *subset-of-subset enumeration*, *3^n enumeration*

> Execute the standard idiom that enumerates every submask of a given mask in descending order, and the paired idiom for iterating supersets. What a learner acquires is the routine itself plus its termination behaviour (why the empty submask needs separate handling, why the loop cannot be written with a naive decrement) — not the argument for why it is exhaustive, which is a separate node.

**Type** — `skill` / `procedural`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-3.bitmask-state-encoding` — Bitmask state encoding

**Required by**

`cl-3.implement-bitmask-dp`, `cl-3.implement-steiner-tree-dp`, `cl-3.subset-cover-transition-correctness`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E4`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — the canonical idiom is a bitwise loop, and JavaScript's 32-bit signed semantics reach into it. The C++ idiom `for (int sub = mask; sub; sub = (sub - 1) & mask)` transcribes to JavaScript character-for-character and works — up to the boundary. At the boundary it fails in a specific way worth writing: if `mask` has bit 31 set, then `(sub - 1) & mask` produces a NEGATIVE int32, which still passes the loop's truthiness guard (so the loop does not hang or stop early) but cannot index the DP table and compares wrongly against other masks — the enumeration proceeds and the lookups are garbage. The C++ version with an unsigned or long long mask has no such boundary. JS-E6 is recorded here only in combination with that specific idiom, per JS-D1: submask enumeration is a 3^n inner loop, so it is a hot path where the boxed-versus-typed table it indexes (JS-E5, recorded on cl-3.implement-bitmask-dp) decides the constant. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation, not a verdict: the idiom relies on `&` and `-` over a machine integer. JavaScript's signed-32-bit bitwise coercion bounds it identically to the encoding node's observation. SUB-8 owns the verdict.

### `cl-3.win-lose-position-semantics`

**Winning and losing position semantics** · aliases: *P-position / N-position*, *win-lose DP*, *game-state DP*

> What it means for a position to be losing under optimal play, stated as the mutual recursion it actually is: a position is losing (P-position) iff EVERY move leads to a winning position, and winning (N-position) iff SOME move leads to a losing one. This is a DP over the game graph whose value is a boolean and whose base case is the terminal position — losing, because the player to move cannot move. The quantifier alternation is the whole content, and it is where the technique's difficulty lives.

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

## CL-4 — DP-optimization (mainstream + research-tier frontier, jointly)

*Techniques whose defining contribution is reducing the cost of evaluating an ALREADY-CORRECT recurrence — the recurrence is given; the contribution is making it affordable.*

**41 nodes** · files: `nodes/cl-4-optimization/mainstream.yaml`, `nodes/cl-4-optimization/frontier.yaml`

### `cl-4.aliens-trick-application`

**Lagrangian / Aliens trick** · aliases: *Aliens trick*, *WQS binary search*, *lambda optimization*, *Lagrangian relaxation DP*

> Drop a DP's count dimension by charging a penalty lambda per constrained use, binary-searching lambda until the unconstrained optimum uses the required count, and correcting the objective back. A learner acquires the search, the recovery of the true answer from the penalised one, and the discipline of checking convexity before trusting any of it.

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.lagrangian-relaxation-for-dp` — Lagrangian relaxation of a counted DP constraint
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.sequence-partition-dp` — Sequence partition DP (index-plus-count state)  *(CL-4→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.aliens-trick-application->cl-1.sequence-partition-dp`)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.aliens-trick-tie-breaking-implementation`, `cl-4.debug-aliens-trick-failure`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| Dimension | Value |

| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |

| `prerequisite_depth` | `5` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-4` |

| `transition_derivation_load` | `4` |

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E2`
- MATERIAL — recorded on NEU-938's evidence, which corrected this audit's first reading. The audit initially marked this neutral by asserting that penalized values sit around n * maxCost ~ 2e14 and are therefore comfortably inside 2^53. The mapper's observation on this node states instead that the penalised objective mixes lambda-scaled counts with costs and ROUTINELY exceeds Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER. On re-examination the audit cannot stand behind its own bound: the penalized value is lambda * count + cost, and BOTH factors are problem-scaled — a cost range at 1e12 with n at 2e5 puts the objective at ~1e17, which is past 2^53, and nothing in the technique caps either factor. C++ carries all of it in long long (exact to ~9.2e18) without comment, which is precisely why the references never mention a bound. Where the penalized objective exceeds 2^53 JavaScript silently rounds it, and because the lambda binary search COMPARES penalized optima, a rounded objective returns the wrong comparison and the search converges on the wrong lambda. Recorded as correctness-risk rather than blocking, and with an uncertainty: whether a given problem's cost scale crosses 2^53 is a per-problem fact this audit cannot enumerate (CAP-2 — no verified problem-level corpus ids, and no benchmarking). The finding is that the C++ default's silent 9.2e18 headroom is not there, and that the technique's arithmetic is problem-scaled rather than bounded — not that any specific problem overflows.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — the penalised objective mixes lambda-scaled counts with costs and routinely exceeds Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER; a real-valued lambda search compounds it. Recorded for SUB-8.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.aliens-trick-convexity-proof`

**Proving the constrained optimum is convex in the count (Aliens trick's licence)** · aliases: *exchange argument for lambda optimization*, *convexity of the k-constrained optimum*

> Establish by exchange argument that the constrained optimum is convex in the constrained count, which is the hypothesis the entire Lagrangian correspondence rests on. A learner acquires the exchange-argument shape and, decisively, the ability to recognize when it does not go through — the case where the trick returns a plausible wrong number rather than failing loudly.

**Type** — `skill` / `proof`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.lagrangian-relaxation-for-dp` — Lagrangian relaxation of a counted DP constraint
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

### `cl-4.aliens-trick-tie-breaking-implementation`

**Aliens-trick tie-breaking and degenerate-segment handling** · aliases: *lambda tie-breaking*, *flat-region handling in WQS binary search*

> Handle the part of the Aliens trick that the theorem does not cover: when f(k) is convex but not strictly convex, a range of counts is optimal at the critical lambda and no lambda selects k exactly. A learner acquires the tie-breaking discipline — track the minimal (or maximal) optimal count consistently, keep lambda integral where costs are, and reconstruct the answer at the boundary — which is the difference between an Aliens-trick solution that is correct and one that is correct on the samples.

- `intra`   → `cl-4.aliens-trick-application` — Lagrangian / Aliens trick

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E9`
- MATERIAL, narrowly — the second of exactly two nodes in the map where JavaScript's floating point is genuinely weaker than C++'s, and, like the first, not for the reason folklore expects. Number is binary64, identical to C++ double, so the lambda binary search itself is neutral. The real gap is JS-E9a: tie-breaking is the whole content of this node — when lambda hits a value where MULTIPLE counts are simultaneously optimal, the degenerate segment must be resolved to recover the intended count, and that means separating penalized values that are equal or nearly so. This is exactly the situation competitive C++ answers by switching double to LONG DOUBLE (x87 80-bit extended) for the extra mantissa bits, or by keeping lambda integral and using __int128 for the penalized comparison. JAVASCRIPT HAS NEITHER PRIMITIVE — binary64 is the ceiling, and there is no FIXED-WIDTH 128-bit integer type. Stated precisely, because the imprecise form would overstate the gap: BigInt does supply exact arbitrary-precision integer arithmetic and can carry the penalized comparison when lambda and the costs are integral, so the remedy is not IMPOSSIBLE in JavaScript — it is a boxed, heap-allocated value rather than a register-width primitive, which makes it a real but COSTLY substitute in a comparison this node runs in its inner loop. So the standard C++ escape hatches for this node's central difficulty do not exist in the one-token form the C++ taxonomy assumes, and the JavaScript author is pushed to resolve the degeneracy structurally instead: keep lambda integral and carry a tie-breaking count alongside the value, comparing lexicographically. A downstream agent inheriting the C++ default would present a one-token remedy JavaScript does not have.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — keeping lambda integral is the standard mitigation for the floating-point form, and JavaScript's single numeric type makes the integral discipline load-bearing rather than optional. Recorded for SUB-8, which owned the verdict and has delivered it.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic`

**Convex Hull Trick (monotonic slopes and queries)** · aliases: *CHT*, *convex hull optimization*, *monotone CHT*

> Maintain the lower envelope of the line family incrementally in a deque, discarding lines that can never again be optimal, so each transition is answered in amortized constant time when both the inserted slopes and the query points are monotone. What a learner acquires is the cost reduction itself — the recurrence, its states, and its answers are identical to the quadratic version.

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.linear-transition-envelope-form` — Linear transitions as a line envelope
- `intra`   → `cl-4.recognize-envelope-structured-transition` — Recognizing an envelope-structured transition
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.convex-hull-envelope-geometry` — Convex-hull / envelope geometry (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 1D sequence DP  *(CL-4→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic->cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp`)*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — CHT's correctness rests on a cross-multiplication that overflows 2^53, and this is invisible unless stated. The hull is maintained by a 'bad' test that asks whether line 2 is ever optimal between lines 1 and 3. The division-free form — the one competitive code uses, precisely to avoid floating-point — is a CROSS-MULTIPLICATION: (b3 - b1) * (m1 - m2) vs (b2 - b1) * (m1 - m3). With slopes and intercepts at the 1e9 scale these problems are posed at, each product reaches ~1e18, which is ~111x past 2^53. C++ evaluates it in long long (exact to ~9.2e18) or reaches for __int128 when even that is tight — the C++ author's whole reason for writing the test this way is that integer arithmetic is EXACT, and in C++ it is. In JavaScript the identical expression silently ROUNDS, so the comparison returns the wrong boolean, so the wrong line is popped, so the hull is silently malformed and the DP returns a wrong answer with no error and no NaN. The irony is exact: the division-free form is chosen to escape floating point, and in JavaScript it lands back in it. A correct JavaScript CHT must do the comparison in BigInt, or split the products, or accept a floating-point test with an epsilon — a decision the C++ author never faces.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only: the "bad line" test compares cross-products that overflow 64-bit in adversarial cases; JavaScript's Number cannot represent those exactly and BigInt changes the constant factor. Flagged for SUB-8; not a verdict.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E9`
- MATERIAL — the differential diagnosis gains a JavaScript-specific branch AND loses a C++ remedy. The node diagnoses an Aliens-trick solution returning a wrong count or a wrong value, whose usual causes (a non-convex objective, a mishandled degenerate segment, a lambda search that terminates early) are language-independent. The JavaScript-specific branch: a wrong count can come from binary64 failing to separate two near-equal penalized candidates in a degenerate segment — a cause that in C++ would have been silently absorbed by a long double the solver reached for, or avoided outright by an __int128 exact comparison (JS-E9a, cl-4.aliens-trick-tie-breaking-implementation). So the JavaScript learner sees a failure the C++ learner would not have seen, AND, having seen it, cannot apply the fix the C++ taxonomy prescribes in the form it prescribes it, because JavaScript has no wider float and no FIXED-WIDTH 128-bit integer. Precisely, as at cl-4.aliens-trick-tie-breaking-implementation: BigInt is exact and can stand in for the __int128 comparison, but it is a boxed arbitrary-precision value rather than a register-width primitive, so it is a costly substitute rather than the same remedy. Both halves change what this node teaches: an extra cause to check, and a remedy to replace.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.divide-and-conquer-optimization`

**Divide-and-conquer optimization** · aliases: *D&C optimization*, *divide and conquer DP optimization*

> Evaluate a layer of a partition DP by solving the middle state first, then recursing left and right with the optimal-split-point range narrowed to [optl, opt(mid)] and [opt(mid), optr]. - **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E8`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the node that WRITES both structures, so it carries the full weight of both hazards plus a container one. JS-E2 is the blocking effect and it is described on cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic (the hull's cross-multiplied bad test) and cl-4.li-chao-tree-dp-application (the midpoint line evaluation); both overflow 2^53 at the 1e9 operand scale these techniques are posed at, both silently round rather than wrapping, and both must be written in BigInt or a split multiply. JS-E8 is the second, independent finding and it is specific to this node: CHT's monotonic form is written in terms of a DEQUE — lines pushed at the back, popped from both ends — and JavaScript has no deque. Array.prototype.shift() is a front removal on a structure that is not a deque, so the C++ idiom deque.pop_front() has no faithful one-line translation; the JavaScript realization is a HEAD/TAIL INDEX PAIR over a preallocated typed array, which also collects JS-E5's payoff. C++ gets std::deque from the stdlib and writes the algorithm directly. A downstream agent must write the container before it can write the technique.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only: this is the node where a JavaScript materiality question is most likely to be real — the integer-safe comparison has no direct Number equivalent. Recorded for SUB-8; this file asserts no verdict.

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — this node writes the DP layer table, and the container decision is JavaScript's. The technique reduces an O(k n^2) DP to O(k n log n), which means it is reached for exactly when k * n is large enough that the quadratic form fails — so the layer tables it fills are big and the per-element cost matters. C++'s vector<long long> is contiguous and unboxed with no decision; the direct JavaScript translation is a boxed Array whose packed-SMI representation is an engine optimization rather than a guarantee. The JavaScript idiom is a flat typed array — Float64Array or Int32Array depending on the cost range, and note the choice is itself constrained by JS-E2 (a cost range past 2^53 has no exact typed-array home at all). JS-E6 is recorded only in combination with that specific mitigable idiom, per JS-D1. Deliberate non-claim on JS-E1: the recursion is O(log n) deep, per cl-4.divide-and-conquer-optimization. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only: the recursion depth is O(log n) and does not stress JavaScript's call stack, unlike some CL-3 recursive DP. Recorded for SUB-8; not a verdict.

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E3`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — this is the node that WRITES the modmul, and it is the densest concentration of JS-E2 in the map. The inner statement is `acc = (acc + a[i][k] * b[k][j]) % M`, executed O(d^3 log k) times. With M = 1e9+7 the product a[i][k] * b[k][j] reaches ~1e18, ~111x past 2^53, so in JavaScript it silently ROUNDS — returning a plausible wrong residue, on essentially every iteration, with no error. C++'s long long is exact and the line is written reflexively. Correct JavaScript needs one of two things, and the choice is a real trade-off a C++ author never makes: BigInt (correct, straightforward, and materially slower at d^3 log k multiplications in the hottest possible loop — JS-E3), or a SPLIT MULTIPLY that decomposes one operand around 2^26 so no partial product leaves the exact range, folding with Math.imul (fast, and easy to get subtly wrong). The addition fold is safe — accumulating d residues below M stays far under 2^53 IF reduced per step — so it is the multiplication alone that must be rewritten, which is exactly why the bug survives casual testing on small moduli. JS-E5 also lands here: the matrix wants a flat Int32Array with i * d + j addressing rather than an array-of-arrays, in an O(d^3) loop. Directional on the performance component only (JS-U2); the correctness component is not directional.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only: the inner multiply-accumulate requires exact products of two values below the modulus, which exceeds JavaScript's safe-integer range for typical 10^9+7 moduli. Recorded for SUB-8; not a verdict.

### `cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-amortization`

**Kinetic-segment-tree certificate amortization** · aliases: *kinetic tournament*, *kinetic segment tree*, *segment tree beats (related family)*

> The fact that a segment tree whose leaves hold linear functions of a global parameter t can maintain their maximum as t advances monotonically, by storing at each node a "certificate" — the value of t at which its winner would change — and rebuilding only the subtrees whose certificates expire; and that although a single advance can cascade, the TOTAL rebuild work over a monotone sweep is bounded (O(n log^2 n) amortized for the standard construction) because each rebuild permanently destroys a crossing that cannot recur. A learner acquires the certificate idea and the amortization CLAIM; arguing the bound is cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-amortization-proof and using it is cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-dp.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.segment-tree` — Segment tree (`data-structure`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

### `cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-dp`

**Kinetic segment tree ↔ DP interplay** · aliases: *kinetic tournament DP*, *kinetic segment tree optimization*

> Evaluate a DP transition that is a maximum (or minimum) over linear functions using a kinetic segment tree, so that the sweep over the DP's index doubles as the monotone advance of the structure's global parameter. A learner acquires the correspondence — DP index becomes kinetic time — and the precondition that makes it available: the parameter must advance MONOTONICALLY, which is what makes this reach the transitions the convex hull trick cannot, at the price of an amortization that a single non-monotone query destroys.

A learner who has not acquired monotonic CHT has no reason for the kinetic structure to exist. ### `cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-implementation`

**Realizing a kinetic segment tree** · aliases: *kinetic tournament implementation*

> Turn the kinetic segment tree into working code: certificates stored per node, an advance routine that recurses only where a certificate has expired, and integer comparison arithmetic in place of the ratio the certificate nominally is. A learner acquires the layout and the arithmetic discipline that keep the amortization real rather than theoretical.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.kinetic-segment-tree-dp` — Kinetic segment tree ↔ DP interplay
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.segment-tree` — Segment tree (`data-structure`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the certificate arithmetic overflows 2^53, exactly as CHT's does and for the same geometric reason. Each node stores a line (slope, intercept) and the tree's whole mechanism is the 'melt time': the parameter value at which the currently-winning line is overtaken by a rival. Computing it is a CROSS-MULTIPLICATION of slope and intercept differences — the same division-free comparison CHT uses, chosen for the same reason (exact integer arithmetic) — and at the 1e9 operand scale these problems are posed at, the products reach ~1e18, ~111x past 2^53. C++ evaluates it in long long or __int128, exactly. JavaScript silently rounds, so a certificate expires at the wrong time or fails to expire, so the tree serves a stale line and the DP is silently wrong — and worse, the AMORTIZED BOUND the structure's whole justification rests on is a counting argument over correct certificate expiries, so a rounded melt time can also break the complexity, not just the answer. Correct JavaScript requires BigInt or split multiplication for the certificate comparison. JS-E5 rides along: the tree is a flat array of nodes and wants typed arrays. Deliberate non-claim on JS-E1: segment-tree recursion is O(log n) deep — about 20 frames — nowhere near the cap.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — the expiry predicate is a cross-multiplied comparison of line coefficients whose products exceed Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER at competitive ranges; BigInt is the obvious mitigation and is slow in an amortized inner loop. Recorded for SUB-8, which owned the verdict and has delivered it.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.knuth-yao-optimization`

**Knuth-Yao optimization** · aliases: *Knuth optimization*, *Knuth-Yao speedup*, *quadrangle inequality optimization*

> Exploit the quadrangle inequality to bound each state's optimal split point between its two neighbours' optima, collapsing an interval DP's inner search from linear to amortized constant across a diagonal. What a learner acquires is the cost reduction; the recurrence and its answers are identical to the cubic version.

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality` — The quadrangle inequality (Monge condition)
- `intra`   → `cl-4.opt-split-point-monotonicity` — Monotonicity of the optimal split point
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-2.formulate-interval-dp` — Formulate an interval DP: choose the endpoints and the joining cost  *(CL-4→CL-2, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.knuth-yao-optimization->cl-2.formulate-interval-dp`)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.select-mainstream-optimization`

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — recorded on this node because Knuth-Yao has no separate implementation node, so this is where the table is written. The optimization takes an interval DP from O(n^3) to O(n^2) by bounding the split search between the two neighbouring optima, which means it is reached for precisely when n is large enough that the cubic form fails — so the n^2 table AND the parallel n^2 opt-index table are both big, both read in the inner loop, and both are where JS-E5's boxed-versus-typed decision lands. C++'s vector<vector<int>> gives unboxed rows for free; the JavaScript idiom is flat Int32Arrays with i * n + j addressing for both tables. The opt-index table is the sharper case: it exists purely to make the inner loop cheap, so paying boxing overhead on it gives back exactly what the optimization bought. JS-E6 is recorded only in combination with that idiom, per JS-D1. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).

### `cl-4.lagrangian-relaxation-for-dp`

**Lagrangian relaxation of a counted DP constraint** · aliases: *Aliens trick*, *lambda optimization*, *parametric search on a DP*, *WQS binary search*

> The fact that a DP constrained to use exactly k of something can be replaced by an unconstrained DP that charges a penalty lambda per use, and that if the constrained optimum f(k) is convex in k then sweeping lambda traces f's lower convex hull — so the constrained answer is recoverable from an unconstrained solve plus the count it happens to use. A learner acquires the reformulation and, inseparably, its precondition: the correspondence is a THEOREM ABOUT CONVEX f, and it is false without convexity. This is the knowledge; applying it is cl-4.aliens-trick-application and establishing convexity is cl-4.aliens-trick-convexity-proof.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

### `cl-4.larsch-online-smawk-implementation`

**Realizing online totally-monotone search (LARSCH) for a self-referential DP** · aliases: *LARSCH algorithm*, *online SMAWK*, *online concave least-weight subsequence*

> Realize totally-monotone row-minima search in the ONLINE setting a real DP forces: where the cost matrix is self-referential — C[i][j] is a function of dp[j], which the same search is in the middle of computing — so the matrix cannot be probed arbitrarily and offline SMAWK is simply unavailable. A learner acquires the interleaving of the search's recursion with the DP's own evaluation order, and the demand discipline that guarantees no entry is read before it exists. This is the deepest point of the mapped frontier: it is where the optimization stops being a subroutine applied to a DP and becomes co-routine with it.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.smawk-application` — SMAWK totally-monotone matrix search
- `intra`   → `cl-4.total-monotonicity` — Total monotonicity of a DP cost matrix
- `intra`   → `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality-proof` — Proving the quadrangle inequality for a cost function
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E6`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL — the node this map's JS-D1 exception exists for, plus a second effect recorded on NEU-938's evidence. JS-E5, from the mapper's observation: the online interleaving carries A PER-LEVEL STACK OF COLUMN ARRAYS under a strict evaluation order, so allocation churn in the hot path is a real realization concern and preallocated typed-array scratch buffers are the JavaScript idiom against a C++ default that gets contiguous vectors for free. The mapper also flags the recursion depth, and this audit RECORDS THAT AS OPEN RATHER THAN ANSWERING IT: LARSCH's online recursion is not the clean halving of offline SMAWK's REDUCE, and the audit cannot establish its depth bound from language semantics alone — it is a property of the algorithm's interleaving, and settling it would mean implementing it, which NEU-941 is barred from. Neither a JS-E1 claim nor a JS-E1 non-claim is asserted here (JS-U5). JS-E6 is the primary effect, and this is the ONE node where it is recorded essentially alone under JS-D1's explicit exception, because LARSCH is the case that exception exists for. LARSCH's entire reason to exist is the CONSTANT FACTOR: it solves the same self-referential totally-monotone DP that a Li Chao tree or a divide-and-conquer scheme also solves, and it is chosen over them ONLY because it is O(n) rather than O(n log n) — a log factor, which is to say a constant-ish factor, bought at a substantial implementation cost. So a language whose constant factor is worse attacks the technique's ONLY advantage, rather than merely taxing it as it taxes everything else. If JavaScript's overhead on LARSCH's intricate stack machinery exceeds the log factor it saves, the technique is not merely slower — it is POINTLESS, and a downstream agent should reach for the simpler O(n log n) structure instead. UNCERTAINTY, EXPLICITLY (JS-U2): whether that is actually the case CANNOT BE ESTABLISHED HERE. It is a quantitative question about a real runtime on a real workload, and implementing or benchmarking JavaScript solutions and selecting a runtime are out of scope for NEU-941 by its own spec. This verdict therefore records the EFFECT AND ITS DIRECTION — JavaScript's constant-factor penalty lands where LARSCH is most vulnerable — and explicitly does NOT assert the conclusion that LARSCH is not worth using in JavaScript. That determination is routed to the curriculum-production charter that owns benchmarking. Recorded as a directional finding with a named uncertainty rather than as either an assertion or a silence.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — the online interleaving is a deep recursion with a per-level stack of column arrays and a strict evaluation order; both the recursion depth and the allocation churn are realization concerns. Recorded for SUB-8, which owned the verdict and has delivered it.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — the same 2^53 ceiling as CHT, reached through a different door. Li Chao trees exist to drop CHT's monotonicity preconditions, and they do it by COMPARING LINE VALUES at a node's midpoint and endpoints: m * x + b, evaluated directly. With slopes at 1e9 and query coordinates at 1e9 — the scale these problems are posed at, because a Li Chao tree is reached for precisely when the coordinates are large and unsorted — the product m * x reaches ~1e18, ~111x past 2^53. C++ evaluates it in long long, exactly. JavaScript silently rounds, so the comparison at the midpoint returns the wrong answer, so the wrong line is kept in the subtree, so the tree is silently wrong. Note this is WORSE than CHT's case in one respect: Li Chao's whole selling point is that it needs no preconditions and is therefore the 'safe' choice a learner is told to reach for when CHT's assumptions are unclear — so the JavaScript author is steered toward it and then broken by it, silently. Correct JavaScript requires BigInt comparison or a split multiply for the line evaluation.

### `cl-4.linear-recurrence-matrix-form`

**Linear recurrences in transfer-matrix form** · aliases: *transfer matrix*, *companion matrix form*

> The fact that a DP whose transition is a fixed linear combination of a bounded number of previous states can be written as repeated multiplication of a state vector by a constant transfer matrix, so the state after n steps is M^n applied to the initial vector. What a learner acquires is the identity between a recurrence and a matrix power — a true statement about the recurrence's algebraic shape, independent of any ability to exploit it.

`cl-4.construct-transfer-matrix`, `cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp`, `cl-4.recognize-matrix-exponentiable-dp`

### `cl-4.linear-transition-envelope-form`

**Linear transitions as a line envelope** · aliases: *line-envelope form of a DP transition*

> The structural fact that a transition of the form dp[i] = min_j (m_j * x_i + c_j) is a pointwise minimum over a family of lines, and is therefore the value of that family's lower envelope evaluated at x_i. What a learner acquires is the identity between an algebraic transition shape and a geometric object — the recurrence is unchanged and still means exactly what it meant; only its readability as geometry is new. This is the fact every Convex Hull Trick and Li Chao node stands on.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.convex-hull-envelope-geometry` — Convex-hull / envelope geometry (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.cht-monotonicity-precondition-proof`, `cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic`, `cl-4.li-chao-tree-dp-application`, `cl-4.recognize-envelope-structured-transition`

### `cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp`

**Matrix-exponentiation DP** · aliases: *matrix power DP*, *linear recurrence acceleration*, *transfer-matrix DP*

> Collapse n sequential DP steps into log n matrix multiplications by exponentiating the transfer matrix, making a recurrence with n up to 10^18 affordable. What a learner acquires is the cost reduction: the recurrence, its states, and its answers are unchanged; only the number of times it must be stepped through moves from linear to logarithmic.

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.linear-recurrence-matrix-form` — Linear recurrences in transfer-matrix form
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.linear-algebra` — Linear algebra (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.modular-arithmetic` — Modular arithmetic (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 1D sequence DP  *(CL-4→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp->cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp`)*
- `cross`   → `cl-3.formulate-automaton-dp` — Formulate a DP over an automaton's states  *(CL-4→CL-3, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp->cl-3.formulate-automaton-dp`)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.construct-transfer-matrix`, `cl-4.recognize-matrix-exponentiable-dp`, `cl-4.select-mainstream-optimization`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E2`, `JS-E3`
- MATERIAL AND BLOCKING — matrix exponentiation is essentially always done UNDER A MODULUS, and that is where JavaScript breaks. The technique's whole purpose is advancing a recurrence 1e18 steps in O(d^3 log k) — and a count advanced 1e18 steps is astronomically large, so the answer is ALWAYS requested modulo a prime (typically 1e9+7). Every cell of every matrix product is therefore a sum of d modular products, and each product of two residues below 1e9+7 reaches ~1e18: ~111x past 2^53. C++'s long long is exact there and the C++ author writes the triple loop without a thought. JavaScript silently rounds every one of them, so the matrix power is wrong from the first multiplication and stays plausible throughout. Note the compounding that makes this worse than a single modmul: the technique performs O(d^3 log k) multiplications, so there is no input small enough for the bug to hide — it fires on essentially every cell. Correct JavaScript requires BigInt (JS-E3, costly at d^3 log k multiplications) or a split multiply. The realization detail is on cl-4.implement-modular-matrix-power (JS-D2); recorded here too because the modulus is constitutive of THIS node — the technique does not exist in the map without it.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only: entries are multiplied under a modulus and products exceed 2^53, so JavaScript Numbers cannot hold intermediate results exactly. Recorded for SUB-8; not a verdict.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.monotonic-deque-maintenance`

**Maintaining a monotonic deque** · aliases: *monotonic queue*, *sliding-window maximum deque*

> Execute the fixed routine that keeps a deque's contents monotone so its front is always the window's extremum: evict dominated elements from the back on insert, evict expired elements from the front on advance. What a learner acquires is a mechanical routine, executed the same way every time, whose amortized O(1) cost is what the surrounding optimization spends.

**Type** — `skill` / `procedural`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.sliding-window-transition-form` — Transitions over a sliding window of states

**Required by**

`cl-4.monotonic-queue-optimization`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| Dimension | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |
| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `3` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `2` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-2` |
| `proof_obligation_load` | `0` |
| `recognition_load` | `1` |
| `state_formulation_load` | `1` |
| `transition_derivation_load` | `1` |

*`creator_review: "deferred-provisional"` — the creator plausibility review did NOT run (charter
Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `idiom-shift` · effects `JS-E8`, `JS-E5`
- MATERIAL — the node is named for a container JavaScript does not have. Maintaining a monotonic deque means pushing at the back, popping dominated elements from the back, and evicting expired elements from the FRONT. C++'s std::deque does all three in O(1) and the algorithm is written directly in its terms. JavaScript has no deque: Array.prototype.shift() is a front removal, but on an Array — it is not a deque operation, it is a re-index of the backing store in the general case, and its behaviour is an engine optimization rather than a guaranteed O(1). So the faithful JavaScript realization is a RING BUFFER OR HEAD/TAIL INDEX PAIR over a preallocated typed array: head and tail integers, push/pop as index arithmetic, no allocation in the loop. That is a container the author must write before writing the technique. It is not merely faster than the shift()-based transcription — it is what makes the O(1) amortized bound the technique's whole analysis rests on actually hold. JS-E5 rides along: the buffer wants an Int32Array. Recorded as idiom-shift rather than performance because the ARTIFACT differs, not just its constant.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Observation only: JavaScript has no native deque; array shift() is O(n) and would silently destroy the amortized bound, so an index-based ring buffer is typically required. Recorded as an observation for SUB-8 — this file asserts no materiality verdict.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.opt-split-point-monotonicity`

**Monotonicity of the optimal split point** · aliases: *argmin monotonicity*, *opt monotonicity*

> The property that the argmin of a transition, opt(i, j), is non-decreasing in j for a fixed layer i. What a learner acquires is the fact itself and what it licenses: that the search for the optimal split of one state constrains the search range of its neighbours, so the total work over a layer telescopes. The recurrence's meaning is untouched; this is a fact about where its optima sit.

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-4.divide-and-conquer-optimization`, `cl-4.knuth-yao-optimization`, `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality`, `cl-4.split-point-monotonicity-proof`

### `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality`

**The quadrangle inequality (Monge condition)** · aliases: *QI*, *Monge condition*, *concave quadrangle inequality*

> The condition C(a, c) + C(b, d) <= C(a, d) + C(b, c) for a <= b <= c <= d, together with what it implies: that a cost function satisfying it forces the DP's optimal split points into the sandwich opt(i, j-1) <= opt(i, j) <= opt(i+1, j). What a learner acquires is the inequality and its consequence — a fact about cost functions, true or false independently of anyone's ability to exploit it.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.opt-split-point-monotonicity` — Monotonicity of the optimal split point
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-4.knuth-yao-optimization`, `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality-proof`, `cl-4.total-monotonicity`

### `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality-proof`

**Proving the quadrangle inequality for a cost function**

> Establish that a specific cost function is Monge — typically by the "sum of a prefix-sum-like weight" closure argument, or by direct algebraic manipulation. What a learner acquires is the ability to know before implementing whether Knuth-Yao is licensed, rather than discovering it from a wrong answer.

### `cl-4.recognize-envelope-structured-transition`

**Recognizing an envelope-structured transition**

> Recognizing that a given correct transition is secretly a query against a family of lines — expanding a squared or product cost until the j-dependent terms separate into a slope and an intercept and the i-dependent term becomes the query point. What a learner acquires is the ability to see the envelope under a surface that does not mention geometry.

**Type** — `skill` / `transfer`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.linear-transition-envelope-form` — Linear transitions as a line envelope
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic`

### `cl-4.recognize-hidden-convexity-for-slope-trick`

**Recognizing hidden convexity that licenses slope trick** · aliases: *spotting slope trick*, *recognizing convex DP value functions*

> The ability to look at a problem statement that mentions no convexity and recognize that its DP value function is convex piecewise-linear in the state parameter — the precondition slope trick needs and the reason slope trick is hard to reach for rather than hard to run. A learner acquires the cues (costs of the form |x - a|, monotonicity constraints being imposed on a sequence, an objective that is a sum of convex terms) and the counter-cues that mean the value function is not convex and slope trick is unavailable.

- `intra`   → `cl-4.slope-trick-convex-function-representation` — Slope-trick representation of a convex piecewise-linear DP value function

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

### `cl-4.select-mainstream-optimization`

**Selecting a mainstream optimization for a too-slow DP**

> Given a correct DP that is too slow, read the recurrence's structural signature and pick the optimization it admits — a line-shaped cost implies an envelope method, monotone argmin implies D&C or Knuth-Yao depending on whether the layers are partitions or intervals, a bounded window implies a monotonic queue, and a huge step count with a small fixed state implies matrix exponentiation. What a learner acquires is the decision itself, which is the ability that makes the other nodes reachable in a contest rather than in hindsight.

**Type** — `skill` / `strategic`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.convex-hull-trick-monotonic` — Convex Hull Trick (monotonic slopes and queries)
- `intra`   → `cl-4.li-chao-tree-dp-application` — Li Chao tree for non-monotone envelope DP
- `intra`   → `cl-4.divide-and-conquer-optimization` — Divide-and-conquer optimization
- `intra`   → `cl-4.knuth-yao-optimization` — Knuth-Yao optimization
- `intra`   → `cl-4.monotonic-queue-optimization` — Monotonic-queue / sliding-window optimization
- `intra`   → `cl-4.matrix-exponentiation-dp` — Matrix-exponentiation DP
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp` — Formulate a 1D sequence DP  *(CL-4→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.select-mainstream-optimization->cl-1.formulate-1d-sequence-dp`)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

| Dimension | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `creator_review` | `deferred-provisional` |
| `dimension_set_version` | `1.0.0` |
| `entry_gate` | `gate-c` |
| `implementation_load` | `1` |
| `prerequisite_depth` | `6` |
| `progression_stage` | `PS-4` |
| `proof_obligation_load` | `1` |

| `state_formulation_load` | `1` |
| `transition_derivation_load` | `2` |

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **JavaScript-neutral** (assessed, explicitly not material)
- JavaScript-neutral. Which optimization a too-slow DP wants is decided by the STRUCTURE of its transition — envelope, monotone split, window, or linear — and that structure is a property of the recurrence, identical in every language. A deliberate non-claim, because a plausible argument runs the other way: several of this cluster's optimizations carry a JavaScript-specific tax (JS-E2's modmul and cross-multiplication rewrites, JS-E8's absent deque), so one could argue the selection calculus shifts. It does not shift enough to be material: the tax applies to the REALIZATION of whichever technique the structure already selected, and does not make a structurally-wrong optimization right or a structurally-right one wrong. Asserting otherwise would be an invented claim.

benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

### `cl-4.sliding-window-transition-form`

**Transitions over a sliding window of states** · aliases: *windowed transition*, *bounded-range transition*

> The structural fact that a transition of the form dp[i] = w_i + min over j in [i-k, i-1] of dp[j] reads a contiguous, forward-moving window of already-computed states, and that consecutive windows overlap in all but their two endpoints. What a learner acquires is the fact that makes incremental extremum maintenance possible at all.

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*

`cl-4.monotonic-deque-maintenance`, `cl-4.monotonic-queue-optimization`, `cl-4.recognize-window-constrained-transition`

### `cl-4.slope-trick-application`

**Slope trick** · aliases: *slope trick*, *the slope trick*, *convex piecewise-linear DP optimization*

> Maintain a DP's convex piecewise-linear value function through the recurrence's transitions by operating on its breakpoint multisets rather than on its values: adding a convex cost term inserts breakpoints, a prefix/suffix minimum deletes the extremal breakpoints on one side, and a shift translates one side. A learner acquires the operation repertoire and which transition shapes map onto which operations — the whole of what makes an O(n * V) tabulation into an O(n log n) algorithm without changing what it computes.

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.slope-trick-convex-function-representation` — Slope-trick representation of a convex piecewise-linear DP value function
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.convex-hull-envelope-geometry` — Convex-hull / envelope geometry (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d` — 2D linear/sequence DP (index-pair state)  *(CL-4→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.slope-trick-application->cl-1.linear-sequence-dp-2d`)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.slope-trick-heap-implementation`, `cl-4.slope-trick-on-trees`

### `cl-4.slope-trick-convex-function-representation`

**Slope-trick representation of a convex piecewise-linear DP value function** · aliases: *slope trick function representation*, *breakpoint multiset representation*

> A convex piecewise-linear function on the integers is determined completely by its minimum value together with the multiset of its slope-change breakpoints — one breakpoint repeated k times means the slope steps by k there. This is the fact slope trick rests on: the DP's value function, which naively costs O(n) to store per state, collapses to two breakpoint multisets (left of the minimum, right of the minimum) plus a scalar. A learner acquires the representation and the correspondence between it and the function it denotes — not yet how to maintain it (cl-4.slope-trick-application) and not yet why the DP's transitions preserve convexity (cl-4.slope-trick-convexity-preservation-proof).

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `anchor`  → `anchor.convex-hull-envelope-geometry` — Convex-hull / envelope geometry (`mathematics`)  *(**SANCTIONED TERMINAL**, register `1.0.0` — not a gap, not decomposed)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.recognize-hidden-convexity-for-slope-trick`, `cl-4.slope-trick-application`, `cl-4.slope-trick-convexity-preservation-proof`

### `cl-4.slope-trick-heap-implementation`

**Realizing slope trick with two lazy heaps** · aliases: *two-heap slope trick*, *lazy-offset slope trick*

> Turn the breakpoint-multiset formulation into code that actually runs in O(n log n): two heaps facing each other across the minimum, lazy add-tags in place of bulk translation, and an accumulator carrying the minimum value that the heaps themselves do not store. A learner acquires the layout and the book-keeping that the mathematical description leaves out — which is where almost every slope-trick implementation is lost.

**Type** — `skill` / `implementation`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.slope-trick-application` — Slope trick
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.implement-memoization-and-tabulation` — Implement a DP both top-down and bottom-up  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.slope-trick-on-trees`

Assumption #11, creator unavailable). **These values ship PROVISIONAL with a named revision trigger.***
*See `../03_open-items-and-provisional-register.md` → deferred creator review.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `correctness-risk` · effects `JS-E8`, `JS-E2`
- MATERIAL on two counts. SECOND COUNT FIRST, recorded on NEU-938's evidence and missed by this audit's first pass (JS-E2): the lazy-offset ACCUMULATOR can exceed Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER on large-coordinate instances. The offsets are added cumulatively across every transition, so the accumulator's range is the SUM of the per-step shifts rather than any single coordinate — it grows with n, and on large-coordinate instances it crosses 2^53 where C++'s long long carries it silently to ~9.2e18. Past that the offset rounds and every breakpoint it is applied to is wrong. FIRST COUNT (JS-E8), the structural one: slope trick is DEFINED in terms of a container JavaScript does not have, and this node is where that is confronted. The canonical formulation is two heaps (a max-heap of left breakpoints, a min-heap of right breakpoints) plus lazy add offsets, and in C++ that formulation IS the code: priority_queue is stdlib, the lazy offset is an integer, and the technique transcribes line for line from the exposition. JavaScript's standard library ships Array, Map, and Set — Set is INSERTION-ORDERED, NOT SORTED — and no binary heap, no priority queue, and no ordered container of any kind (JS-E8). So every JavaScript slope-trick solution must hand-roll a binary heap before it can write a line of slope trick. That is a real, separable prerequisite acquisition that the C++ learner simply does not have, and it materially changes what a downstream agent authors on this node: the exercise is 'implement a heap, then implement slope trick', not 'implement slope trick'. The lazy-offset discipline compounds it slightly — the offset must be applied on push and pop of a heap the author wrote, rather than of one they trust. SEVERITY, under the aggregation rule recorded as D-R2 in the adjudication ledger — a node's severity is the most severe of the severities its individual effects earn under the written tests — is the maximum of two per-effect scores. JS-E8 earns IDIOM-SHIFT on its own test: a hand-rolled binary heap over a typed array is entirely feasible and the asymptotics are unchanged, so that gap is one of container idiom. JS-E2 earns CORRECTNESS-RISK on its own test: the accumulator is correct at the sizes slope trick usually meets, but a reachable large-coordinate range carries it past 2^53, and past that the offset rounds silently. The maximum is CORRECTNESS-RISK, which is what this node now carries — consistent with the single-effect JS-E2 precedent on cl-4.aliens-trick-application. The earlier idiom-shift verdict scored the container gap alone and left the accumulator's 2^53 hazard out of the severity even though this rationale already stated it.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — two heaps with lazy offsets and an accumulator; JavaScript has no standard-library heap, and the accumulator can exceed Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER on large-coordinate instances. Recorded for SUB-8, which owned the verdict and has delivered it.

*`JS-U2` (inherited, open): **every performance verdict is directional, never quantified.** No
benchmark was run. Do not read a `performance` severity as a measured factor.*

**Coverage** — node block (NEU-934–938); map-level verdict NEU-942

- `status: "unaudited"` · `corpus_refs`: `C5`, `C4`
- *`unaudited` is **accurate, not an omission**: NEU-942 adjudicated coverage at the **map** level (30
  disagreements, zero smoothed) and **wrote no node file** (its `AC-6-C`), so no node-level verdict was
  ever written back. The binding coverage verdict for this technique is "**in scope and mapped; the OUT-7
  sweep raised no gap against it**". Recorded as `INC-C7` in `../06_caps-and-incomplete-scope.md` —

### `cl-4.slope-trick-on-trees`

**Slope trick on trees (mergeable breakpoint multisets)** · aliases: *tree slope trick*, *mergeable slope trick*, *slope trick with small-to-large*

> Extend slope trick from a linear scan to a tree by making the breakpoint multiset mergeable — combining a vertex's children's functions with a mergeable heap or a small-to-large union, then applying the vertex's own convex cost. A learner acquires the merge discipline and the amortization that keeps it affordable, which is what separates this from "run slope trick per root-to-leaf path" (which is not correct) and from "merge naively" (which is not fast).

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.slope-trick-application` — Slope trick
- `intra`   → `cl-4.slope-trick-heap-implementation` — Realizing slope trick with two lazy heaps
- `intra`   → `cl-4.slope-trick-convexity-preservation-proof` — Proving a DP's transitions preserve convexity (slope trick's licence)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-2.formulate-subtree-recurrence` — Formulate a subtree recurrence: decide what a subtree must summarize  *(CL-4→CL-2, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.slope-trick-on-trees->cl-2.formulate-subtree-recurrence`)*

**Required by**

*nothing — this is a leaf of the current map.*

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `blocking` · effects `JS-E8`, `JS-E1`
- MATERIAL — the most severe container gap in the map, and it meets a second effect on the way. The technique merges children's breakpoint multisets up a tree, and its efficiency rests on SMALL-TO-LARGE merging of an ORDERED MULTISET — C++'s std::multiset, with the standard trick of merging the smaller into the larger and stealing the larger's storage — or on a mergeable heap (leftist/skew). JavaScript has NEITHER: no ordered multiset, no lower_bound, and no mergeable heap (JS-E8). So the JavaScript author must hand-roll not the binary heap of cl-4.slope-trick-heap-implementation but a MERGEABLE one — a leftist or skew heap, which is a materially harder structure and a genuinely separate acquisition — or hand-roll an ordered multiset with the small-to-large merge, whose complexity argument depends on properties of the structure they wrote. JS-E1 is the second effect: the merge is driven by a post-order tree traversal, which at competitive tree sizes exceeds JavaScript's ~1e4 frame cap on a path-shaped tree and must be an explicit stack (cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs) — and the explicit-stack rewrite is harder here than usual, because each frame must carry a heap handle. SEVERITY, under the aggregation rule recorded as D-R2 in the adjudication ledger — a node's severity is the most severe of the severities its individual effects earn under the written tests — is the maximum of two per-effect scores. JS-E8 earns IDIOM-SHIFT on its own test: the mergeable structure is hand-rollable and correct, so that gap is one of container idiom, and it is the widest such gap in the map. JS-E1 earns BLOCKING on its own test: the direct transcription of the recursive post-order crashes with RangeError at competitive tree sizes, and an explicit stack is REQUIRED, NOT PREFERRED. The maximum is BLOCKING, which is what this node now carries — consistent with the single-effect JS-E1 precedent on cl-2.implement-tree-dp-post-order-dfs, the very node this rationale cross-references. RULING ON THE EARLIER SURMOUNTABILITY CRITERION, recorded rather than dropped: this node previously read idiom-shift rather than blocking BECAUSE BOTH OBSTACLES ARE SURMOUNTABLE. That criterion is OVERRIDDEN, and the ruling is made where it belongs — at JS-E1's own per-effect score, never as a downgrade applied after aggregation, because the rule has no downgrade step and a post-hoc downgrade would subtract from a maximum the rule itself fixed. Surmountability is a claim about DIFFICULTY, while the blocking test asks about NECESSITY (required, not preferred); and since the effect catalogue names a JavaScript technique that recovers every effect, letting surmountability lower a score would leave blocking with no node it could ever apply to. The obstacles ARE surmountable — that remains true, and it is why this is a large acquisition rather than a dead end — but it does not lower the score.
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — a mergeable heap (leftist/skew/pairing) has no JavaScript standard-library equivalent, and the recursion is over a tree that may be deep. Recorded for SUB-8.

### `cl-4.smawk-application`

**SMAWK totally-monotone matrix search** · aliases: *SMAWK algorithm*, *totally monotone matrix searching*, *linear-time row minima*

> Compute all row minima of a totally monotone matrix in time linear in its dimensions, via SMAWK's alternating REDUCE step (discard columns that can hold no row minimum, using total monotonicity to prove they cannot) and INTERPOLATE step (recurse on even rows, then fill odd rows within the bounds their neighbours establish). A learner acquires the recursion and the reason it is linear rather than log-linear — the strict improvement over the D&C optimization that exploits the same hypothesis less aggressively.

**Type** — `skill` / `optimization`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.total-monotonicity` — Total monotonicity of a DP cost matrix
- `intra`   → `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality-proof` — Proving the quadrangle inequality for a cost function

- `root`    → `cl-1.root.formulate-state-transition-base-case` — Formulate a DP: design the state, derive the transition, fix the base cases  *(floor terminal)*
- `cross`   → `cl-1.sequence-partition-dp` — Sequence partition DP (index-plus-count state)  *(CL-4→CL-1, realized by NEU-939, `xce.cl-4.smawk-application->cl-1.sequence-partition-dp`)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.larsch-online-smawk-implementation`

**JavaScript materiality** — NEU-941, `rule_version 1.0.0`

- **MATERIAL** · severity `performance` · effects `JS-E5`, `JS-E6`
- MATERIAL — recorded on NEU-938's evidence, which found the one hazard this audit's first pass missed. Two expected hazards genuinely do NOT apply, and both non-claims are kept: SMAWK's REDUCE recurses on a halved problem, so the depth is O(log n) — about 20 frames against a cap on the order of 1e4, so JS-E1 does not bind; and its arithmetic is COMPARISONS of matrix entries generated on demand, with no multiplication to overflow 2^53, so JS-E2 does not bind. The audit's first pass then concluded neutral by reasoning that the matrix is implicit and never materialized, so there is no table for JS-E5. The mapper's observation on this node shows that reasoning is incomplete: SMAWK's access pattern is A STACK OF SHRINKING COLUMN-INDEX ARRAYS — REDUCE builds a new surviving-column list at every level — so while the MATRIX is implicit, the column-index arrays are real, allocated per level, and are the algorithm's actual working set. Typed arrays (or a single preallocated scratch buffer indexed per level) are the JavaScript idiom; a per-level Array.push loop is allocation churn in the hot path. And the mapper's second point sharpens why it matters: SMAWK's constant factor IS its selling point over divide-and-conquer optimization — the same reasoning that puts cl-4.larsch-online-smawk-implementation under JS-D1's exception applies here in weaker form. Recorded with JS-E5's specific mitigable idiom rather than as bare JS-E6, per JS-D1. Directional, not quantified (JS-U2).
- *mapper note (preserved):* Pre-verdict observation, retained as the record of what the mapper saw before SUB-8 ruled — SUB-8 HAS SINCE DELIVERED the verdict in the fields above, so this note supports that verdict and no longer withholds one — SMAWK's constant factor is its whole selling point over D&C optimization, and its access pattern is a stack of shrinking column-index arrays — typed arrays are the obvious mitigation. Recorded for SUB-8, which owned the verdict and has delivered it.

### `cl-4.total-monotonicity`

**Total monotonicity of a DP cost matrix** · aliases: *totally monotone matrix*, *total monotonicity*, *row-minima monotonicity*

> The fact that a cost matrix can be TOTALLY MONOTONE — stated as a comparison between two entries, NOT in terms of the row minima the property is meant to explain: for any rows i1 < i2 and columns j1 < j2, if C[i1][j1] > C[i1][j2] then C[i2][j1] > C[i2][j2] (if an upper row strictly prefers the right-hand column, every lower row prefers it too) — and that total monotonicity IMPLIES the ROW-MINIMUM POSITIONS are non-decreasing down the rows. Definition first, row-minima consequence second, and in that order on purpose: the consequence is precisely what SMAWK exploits, so it has to be DERIVED from the property rather than built into its statement. This is the exact hypothesis SMAWK needs, and it is STRICTLY WEAKER than the Monge condition: Monge implies total monotonicity, not conversely. A learner acquires the property, the row-minima consequence, and the implication's DIRECTION — which is what lets them see that establishing Monge is a sufficient route to SMAWK's hypothesis rather than the hypothesis itself.

**Type** — `knowledge`

**Requires**

- `intra`   → `cl-4.quadrangle-inequality` — The quadrangle inequality (Monge condition)
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.state-transition-base-case-formulation` — State, transition, and base-case formulation  *(floor terminal)*
- `root`    → `cl-1.root.optimal-substructure` — Optimal substructure  *(floor terminal)*

**Required by**

`cl-4.larsch-online-smawk-implementation`, `cl-4.smawk-application`

**Stage / difficulty** — NEU-940, `dimension_set_version 1.0.0`

*Notes (verbatim from the owning mapper):* X-938-2 RESOLVED — THIS NODE WAS NARROWED AFTER mainstream.yaml MERGED. SUB-13 originally wrote this node as "Total monotonicity AND the Monge condition", which predicted a duplicate against SUB-6. That prediction landed: NEU-937 merged cl-4.quadrangle-inequality ("The quadrangle inequality (Monge condition)", aliases incl. "Monge condition"). Rather than ship two CL-4 knowledge nodes for the same fact, this node now states ONLY total monotonicity — the strictly weaker property SMAWK actually needs — and DRAWS the Monge condition from SUB-6's node via prerequisites.intra_cluster. That edge is intra-cluster and now RESOLVES against a real merged id (both CL-4 files share one cluster and one namespace, header rule 2b). THIS IS NOT A MERGE OF THE TWO NODES AND NOT A DELETION OF SUB-6's. It is the mathematically honest split, and the split is real rather than diplomatic: Monge implies total monotonicity but NOT conversely, and SUB-6's node states Monge's SPLIT-POINT-SANDWICH consequence (what Knuth-Yao and D&C optimization need) while SMAWK needs the ROW-MINIMA consequence of the weaker property. Two different facts, correctly two nodes, with the implication drawn as the edge it is. NO BOUNDARY ANCHOR IS DRAWN AND NO AR-1 IS FILED, deliberately. Total monotonicity is a property of a matrix, so one might argue it is non-DP and wants an anchor. Rejected: the register's rule (boundary-register.yaml §boundary) admits an anchor iff it is (a) NOT DP, (b) a real prerequisite of a DP technique, (c) above the elementary floor. (a) fails in the sense that matters — the object is THIS DP's cost matrix and the property is asserted OF the recurrence's own transition cost, which is DP content, not borrowed general-algorithms content. SUB-6 reached the same disposition independently for cl-4.quadrangle-inequality (no anchor, roots only), which is corroboration rather than coincidence.
